### §25 Nirvāṇaparīkṣā (Examination of extinguishment)

<Tabs syncKey="sanskrit">
<TabItem label="Translation">
**25.1** If all this is empty, there is no arising and no passing away; \
through abandoning or through cessation of what is `nirvāṇa` (extinguishment) accepted[^244]?

**25.2** If all this is not empty, there is no arising and no passing away; \
through abandoning or through cessation of what is `nirvāṇa` accepted?

**25.3** Not abandoned, not attained, not annihilated, not eternal, \
not ceased, not arisen: this is called `nirvāṇa`.

**25.4** To begin with, `nirvāṇa` is not an existent: having `jarāmaraṇa` (ageing and death) as its characteristic would follow; \
for there is no existent without `jarāmaraṇa`.

**25.5** And if `nirvāṇa` were an existent, `nirvāṇa` would be `saṃskṛta` (constructed); \
for no `asaṃskṛta` (unconstructed) existent whatsoever is found anywhere.

**25.6** And if `nirvāṇa` were an existent, how could that `nirvāṇa` be without taking up?[^245] \
For no existent at all is found without taking up.

**25.7** If `nirvāṇa` is not an existent, will `nirvāṇa` be a non-existent?[^246] \
Where there is no existent, there no non-existent is found.

**25.8** And if `nirvāṇa` were a non-existent, how could that `nirvāṇa` be without taking up? \
For there is no non-existent that is found without taking up.

**25.9** Whatever state of moving to and fro there is, taking up or depending, \
that, not depending and not taking up, is taught to be `nirvāṇa`[^247].

**25.10** And the Teacher spoke of the abandoning of `bhava` (existence) and of passing away; \
therefore it is fitting that `nirvāṇa` is not an existent and not a non-existent.

**25.11** If `nirvāṇa` were both, a non-existent and an existent, \
release would be a non-existent and an existent, and that is not fitting.

**25.12** If `nirvāṇa` were both, a non-existent and an existent, \
`nirvāṇa` would not be without taking up; for both of those take up[^248].

**25.13** How could `nirvāṇa` be both, a non-existent and an existent? \
For `nirvāṇa` is `asaṃskṛta`, while the existent and the non-existent are `saṃskṛta`.

**25.14** How could `nirvāṇa` be both, a non-existent and an existent? \
For the non-existence of those two in one place is like that of light and darkness[^249].

**25.15** The making clear that `nirvāṇa` is neither a non-existent nor an existent, \
that is established only when a non-existent and an existent are both established.

**25.16** If `nirvāṇa`, neither a non-existent nor an existent, is found, \
by what is that[^250] "neither a non-existent nor an existent"[^251] made clear?

**25.17** It is not made clear at all[^252] that the Bhagavān exists after cessation; \
nor is "he does not exist", nor "both", nor "not both" made clear.

**25.18** Even while the Bhagavān remains, it is not made clear at all that he exists; \
nor is "he does not exist", nor "both", nor "not both" made clear.

**25.19** There is no distinction at all of `saṃsāra` (cycle of rebirth) from `nirvāṇa`; \
there is no distinction at all of `nirvāṇa` from `saṃsāra`.

**25.20** And what is the limit of `nirvāṇa` and the limit of cycling through rebirth, \
between those two no difference at all[^253], not even a very subtle one, is found.

**25.21** The `dṛṣṭis` (views) "after cessation", "finite" and so on, and "eternal" and so on, \
rest upon `nirvāṇa`, upon the future and upon the past[^254].

**25.22** When all `dharmas` (phenomena) are empty, what is infinite, what is finite[^255], \
what is both infinite and finite, and what is neither infinite nor finite?

**25.23** What is just that, what is other, what is eternal, what is not eternal, \
what is both not eternal and eternal, or what, then, is not both?

**25.24** The quieting of all apprehension, the auspicious quieting of `prapañca` (proliferation): \
no `dharma` was taught by the Buddha anywhere to anyone.[^256]

</TabItem>

<TabItem label="Sanskrit (Roman IAST)">
**25.1** yadi śūnyamidaṃ sarvamudayo nāsti na vyayaḥ | \
prahāṇād vā nirodhād vā kasya nirvāṇamiṣyate ||

**25.2** yadyaśūnyamidaṃ sarvamudayo nāsti na vyayaḥ | \
prahāṇād vā nirodhād vā kasya nirvāṇamiṣyate ||

**25.3** aprahīṇamasaṃprāptamanucchinnamaśāśvatam | \
aniruddhamanutpannametannirvāṇamucyate ||

**25.4** bhāvastāvanna nirvāṇaṃ jarāmaraṇalakṣaṇam | \
prasajyetāsti bhāvo hi na jarāmaraṇaṃ vinā ||

**25.5** bhāvaśca yadi nirvāṇaṃ nirvāṇaṃ saṃskṛtaṃ bhavet | \
nāsaṃskṛto hi vidyate bhāvaḥ kvacana kaścana ||

**25.6** bhāvaśca yadi nirvāṇamanupādāya tat katham | \
nirvāṇaṃ nānupādāya kaścid bhāvo hi vidyate ||

**25.7** bhāvo yadi na nirvāṇamabhāvaḥ kiṃ bhaviṣyati | \
nirvāṇaṃ yatra bhāvo na nābhāvastatra vidyate ||

**25.8** yadyabhāvaśca nirvāṇamanupādāya tat katham | \
nirvāṇaṃ na hyabhāvo’sti yo’nupādāya vidyate ||

**25.9** ya ājavaṃjavībhāva upādāya pratītya vā | \
so’pratītyānupādāya nirvāṇamupadiśyate ||

**25.10** prahāṇaṃ cābravīcchāstā bhavasya vibhavasya ca | \
tasmānna bhāvo nābhāvo nirvāṇamiti yujyate ||

**25.11** bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ yadi | \
bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca mokṣastacca na yujyate ||

**25.12** bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ yadi | \
nānupādāya nirvāṇamupādāyobhayaṃ hi tat ||

**25.13** bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ katham | \
asaṃskṛtaṃ hi nirvāṇaṃ bhāvābhāvau hi saṃskṛtau ||

**25.14** bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ katham | \
tayorabhāvo hyekatra prakāśatamasoriva ||

**25.15** naivābhāvo naiva bhāvo nirvāṇamiti yāñjanā | \
abhāve caiva bhāve ca sā siddhe sati sidhyati ||

**25.16** naivābhāvo naiva bhāvo nirvāṇaṃ yadi vidyate | \
naivabhāvo naiva bhāva iti kena tadajyate ||

**25.17** paraṃ nirodhād bhagavān bhavatītyeva nājyate | \
na bhavatyubhayaṃ ceti nobhayaṃ ceti nājyate ||

**25.18** tiṣṭhamāno’pi bhagavān bhavatītyeva nājyate | \
na bhavatyubhayaṃ ceti nobhayaṃ ceti nājyate ||

**25.19** na saṃsārasya nirvāṇāt kiṃcidasti viśeṣaṇam | \
na nirvāṇasya saṃsārāt kiṃcidasti viśeṣaṇam ||

**25.20** nirvāṇasya ca yā koṭiḥ koṭiḥ saṃsaraṇasya ca | \
na tayorantaraṃ kiṃcit susūkṣmamapi vidyate ||

**25.21** paraṃ nirodhādantādyāḥ śāśvatādyāśca dṛṣṭayaḥ | \
nirvāṇamaparāntaṃ ca pūrvāntaṃ ca samāśritāḥ ||

**25.22** śūnyeṣu sarvadharmeṣu kimanantaṃ kimantavat | \
kimanantamantavacca nānantaṃ nāntavacca kim ||

**25.23** kiṃ tadeva kimanyat kiṃ śāśvataṃ kimaśāśvatam | \
aśāśvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca kiṃ vā nobhayamapyatha ||

**25.24** sarvopalambhopaśamaḥ prapañcopaśamaḥ śivaḥ | \
na kvacit kasyacit kaścit dharmo buddhena deśitaḥ ||

an existent | if | not | extinguishment | a non-existent | is it that | will it be | extinguishment | where | an existent | not | no | a non-existent | there | is found  
If `nirvāṇa` is not an existent, will `nirvāṇa` be a non-existent?  
Where there is no existent, there no non-existent is found.

25.8 yadyabhāvaśca nirvāṇamanupādāya tat katham nirvāṇaṃ na hyabhāvo’sti yo’nupādāya vidyate  
⏏️(yadi) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) 🔽(ya)🆎(na+upādadāti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) ⏏️(katham) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(hi) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(asti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(yad) 🔽(ya)🆎(na+upādadāti) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(vidyate)  
if | a non-existent | and | extinguishment | without taking up | that | how | extinguishment | not | for | a non-existent | there is | which | without taking up | is found  
And if `nirvāṇa` were a non-existent, how could that `nirvāṇa` be without taking up?  
For there is no non-existent that is found without taking up.

25.9 ya ājavaṃjavībhāva upādāya pratītya vā so’pratītyānupādāya nirvāṇamupadiśyate  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(yad) 🚹⨀①(ājavaṃjavībhāva) 🔽(ya)(upādadāti) 🔽(ya)(pratyeti) ⏏️(vā) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) 🔽(ya)🆎(na+pratyeti) 🔽(ya)🆎(na+upādadāti) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upadiśyate)  
whatever | state of moving to and fro | taking up | depending | or | that | not depending | not taking up | extinguishment | is taught  
Whatever state of moving to and fro there is, taking up or depending,  
that, not depending and not taking up, is taught to be `nirvāṇa`.

25.10 prahāṇaṃ cābravīcchāstā bhavasya vibhavasya ca tasmānna bhāvo nābhāvo nirvāṇamiti yujyate  
🚻⨀②(prahāṇa) ⏏️(ca) 🟢⨀🤟↩️(bravīti) 🚹⨀①(śāstṛ) 🚹⨀⑥(bhava) 🚹⨀⑥(vibhava) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(tasmāt) ⏏️(na) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(na) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) ⏏️(iti) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
the abandoning | and | spoke of | the Teacher | of existence | of passing away | and | therefore | not | an existent | not | a non-existent | extinguishment | thus | is fitting  
And the Teacher spoke of the abandoning of `bhava` (existence) and of passing away;  
therefore it is fitting that `nirvāṇa` is not an existent and not a non-existent.

25.11 bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ yadi bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca mokṣastacca na yujyate  
🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(yadi) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①(mokṣa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
would be | a non-existent | an existent | and | extinguishment | both | if | would be | a non-existent | an existent | and | release | that | and | not | is fitting  
If `nirvāṇa` were both, a non-existent and an existent,  
release would be a non-existent and an existent, and that is not fitting.

25.12 bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ yadi nānupādāya nirvāṇamupādāyobhayaṃ hi tat  
🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(yadi) ⏏️(na) 🔽(ya)🆎(na+upādadāti) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) 🔽(ya)(upādadāti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(hi) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad)  
would be | a non-existent | an existent | and | extinguishment | both | if | not | without taking up | extinguishment | taking up | both | for | that  
If `nirvāṇa` were both, a non-existent and an existent,  
`nirvāṇa` would not be without taking up; for both of those take up.

25.13 bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ katham asaṃskṛtaṃ hi nirvāṇaṃ bhāvābhāvau hi saṃskṛtau  
🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(katham) 🚻⨀①🔽(ta)🆎(na+saṃskṛta) ⏏️(hi) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) 🚹⨁①🆎(bhāva+abhāva) ⏏️(hi) 🚹⨁①🔽(ta)(saṃskṛta)  
could be | a non-existent | an existent | and | extinguishment | both | how | unconstructed | for | extinguishment | the existent and the non-existent | indeed | constructed  
How could `nirvāṇa` be both, a non-existent and an existent?  
For `nirvāṇa` is `asaṃskṛta`, while the existent and the non-existent are `saṃskṛta`.

25.14 bhavedabhāvo bhāvaśca nirvāṇamubhayaṃ katham tayorabhāvo hyekatra prakāśatamasoriva  
🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(katham) *️⃣🤟🚹⨁⑥(tad) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) ⏏️(hi) ⏏️(ekatra) 🚻⨁⑥🆎(prakāśa+tamas) ⏏️(iva)  
could be | a non-existent | an existent | and | extinguishment | both | how | of those two | the non-existence | for | in one place | of light and darkness | like  
How could `nirvāṇa` be both, a non-existent and an existent?  
For the non-existence of those two in one place is like that of light and darkness.

25.15 naivābhāvo naiva bhāvo nirvāṇamiti yāñjanā abhāve caiva bhāve ca sā siddhe sati sidhyati  
⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤟🚺⨀①(yad) 🚺⨀①(añjanā) 🚹⨀⑦(abhāva) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀⑦(bhāva) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚺⨀①(tad) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(ta)(siddha) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(at)(sat) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(sidhyati)  
not | at all | a non-existent | not | at all | an existent | extinguishment | thus | which | the making clear | a non-existent | and | only | an existent | and | that | established | being | is established  
The making clear that `nirvāṇa` is neither a non-existent nor an existent,  
that is established only when a non-existent and an existent are both established.

25.16 naivābhāvo naiva bhāvo nirvāṇaṃ yadi vidyate naivabhāvo naiva bhāva iti kena tadajyate  
⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) 🚻⨀①(nirvāṇa) ⏏️(yadi) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(vidyate) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(abhāva) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(bhāva) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀③(kim) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(ajyate)  
not | at all | a non-existent | not | at all | an existent | extinguishment | if | is found | not | at all | a non-existent | not | at all | an existent | thus | by what | that | is made clear  
If `nirvāṇa`, neither a non-existent nor an existent, is found,  
by what is that "neither a non-existent nor an existent" made clear?

25.17 paraṃ nirodhād bhagavān bhavatītyeva nājyate na bhavatyubhayaṃ ceti nobhayaṃ ceti nājyate  
⏏️(param) 🚹⨀⑤(nirodha) 🚹⨀①(bhagavat) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(eva) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(ajyate) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(ajyate)  
after | cessation | the Bhagavān | exists | thus | at all | not | is made clear | not | exists | both | and | thus | not | both | and | thus | not | is made clear  
It is not made clear at all that the Bhagavān exists after cessation;  
nor is "he does not exist", nor "both", nor "not both" made clear.

25.18 tiṣṭhamāno’pi bhagavān bhavatītyeva nājyate na bhavatyubhayaṃ ceti nobhayaṃ ceti nājyate  
🚹⨀①🔽(māna)(tiṣṭhamāna) ⏏️(api) 🚹⨀①(bhagavat) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(eva) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(ajyate) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(ajyate)  
remaining | even | the Bhagavān | exists | thus | at all | not | is made clear | not | exists | both | and | thus | not | both | and | thus | not | is made clear  
Even while the Bhagavān remains, it is not made clear at all that he exists;  
nor is "he does not exist", nor "both", nor "not both" made clear.

25.19 na saṃsārasya nirvāṇāt kiṃcidasti viśeṣaṇam na nirvāṇasya saṃsārāt kiṃcidasti viśeṣaṇam  
⏏️(na) 🚹⨀⑥(saṃsāra) 🚻⨀⑤(nirvāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kaścit) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(asti) 🚻⨀①(viśeṣaṇa) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀⑥(nirvāṇa) 🚹⨀⑤(saṃsāra) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kaścit) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(asti) 🚻⨀①(viśeṣaṇa)  
not | of the cycle of rebirth | from extinguishment | any | is | distinction | not | of extinguishment | from the cycle of rebirth | any | is | distinction  
There is no distinction at all of `saṃsāra` (cycle of rebirth) from `nirvāṇa`;  
there is no distinction at all of `nirvāṇa` from `saṃsāra`.

25.20 nirvāṇasya ca yā koṭiḥ koṭiḥ saṃsaraṇasya ca na tayorantaraṃ kiṃcit susūkṣmamapi vidyate  
🚻⨀⑥(nirvāṇa) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚺⨀①(yad) 🚺⨀①(koṭi) 🚺⨀①(koṭi) 🚻⨀⑥(saṃsaraṇa) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚺⨁⑥(tad) 🚻⨀①(antara) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kaścit) 🚻⨀①(susūkṣma) ⏏️(api) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(vidyate)  
of extinguishment | and | which | limit | limit | of cycling through rebirth | and | not | of those two | difference | any | very subtle | even | is found  
And what is the limit of `nirvāṇa` and the limit of cycling through rebirth,  
between those two no difference at all, not even a very subtle one, is found.

25.21 paraṃ nirodhādantādyāḥ śāśvatādyāśca dṛṣṭayaḥ nirvāṇamaparāntaṃ ca pūrvāntaṃ ca samāśritāḥ  
⏏️(param) 🚹⨀⑤(nirodha) 🚺⨂①🆎(anta+ādi) 🚺⨂①🆎(śāśvata+ādi) ⏏️(ca) 🚺⨂①(dṛṣṭi) 🚻⨀②(nirvāṇa) 🚹⨀②(aparānta) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀②(pūrvānta) ⏏️(ca) 🚺⨂①🔽(ta)(samāśrita)  
after | cessation | 'finite' and so on | 'eternal' and so on | and | views | extinguishment | the future | and | the past | and | rest upon  
The `dṛṣṭis` (views) "after cessation", "finite" and so on, and "eternal" and so on,  
rest upon `nirvāṇa`, upon the future and upon the past.

25.22 śūnyeṣu sarvadharmeṣu kimanantaṃ kimantavat kimanantamantavacca nānantaṃ nāntavacca kim  
🚹⨂⑦(śūnya) 🚹⨂⑦🆎(sarva+dharma) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+anta) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) 🚻⨀①(antavat) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+anta) 🚻⨀①(antavat) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+anta) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀①(antavat) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim)  
when empty | all phenomena | what | infinite | what | finite | what | infinite | finite | and | not | infinite | not | finite | and | what  
When all `dharmas` (phenomena) are empty, what is infinite, what is finite,  
what is both infinite and finite, and what is neither infinite nor finite?

25.23 kiṃ tadeva kimanyat kiṃ śāśvataṃ kimaśāśvatam aśāśvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca kiṃ vā nobhayamapyatha  
*️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) ⏏️(eva) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(anya) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(kim) ⏏️(vā) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(api) ⏏️(atha)  
what | that | just | what | other | what | eternal | what | not eternal | not eternal | eternal | and | what | or | not | both | also | then  
What is just that, what is other, what is eternal, what is not eternal,  
what is both not eternal and eternal, or what, then, is not both?

25.24 sarvopalambhopaśamaḥ prapañcopaśamaḥ śivaḥ na kvacit kasyacit kaścit dharmo buddhena deśitaḥ  
🚹⨀①🆎(sarva+upalambha+upaśama) 🚹⨀①🆎(prapañca+upaśama) 🚹⨀①(śiva) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(kvacit) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀⑥(kaścit) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(kaścit) 🚹⨀①(dharma) 🚹⨀③(buddha) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)(deśita)  
the quieting of all apprehension | the quieting of proliferation | auspicious | not | anywhere | to anyone | any | dharma | by the Buddha | taught  
The quieting of all apprehension, the auspicious quieting of `prapañca` (proliferation):  
no `dharma` was taught by the Buddha anywhere to anyone.

### §26 Dvādaśāṅgaparīkṣā (Examination of the twelve links)

<Tabs syncKey="sanskrit">
<TabItem label="Translation">
**26.1** For appearing again, one covered by `avidyā` (ignorance) constructs the `saṃskāras` (mental constructions)[^257] in three ways, \
and by those `karmans` (actions) one goes to a destination.

**26.2** `vijñāna` (consciousness), with the `saṃskāras` as its cause, settles into the destination; \
then, when `vijñāna` has settled, `nāmarūpa` (phenomenal objects) is infused.

**26.3** But when `nāmarūpa` has been infused, there is the coming to be of the six sense bases; \
owing to the six sense bases[^258], contact proceeds.

**26.4** Depending on the eye and on form, and on attention too, \
thus, depending on `nāmarūpa`, `vijñāna` proceeds.

**26.5** The coming together of the three, form, `vijñāna` and the eye, \
that is contact; and from that contact, feeling proceeds.

**26.6** `tṛṣṇā` (craving) has feeling as its cause, for one craves for the sake of feeling[^259]; \
one who is craving takes up the fourfold `upādāna` (fuel)[^260].

**26.7** When there is `upādāna`, the `bhava` (existence) of the taker of fuel proceeds[^261]; \
for if one were without `upādāna`, one would be released; there would be no `bhava`.

**26.8** And that `bhava` is the five `skandhas` (collections); from `bhava`, `jāti` (birth) proceeds; \
`jarāmaraṇa` (ageing and death), `duḥkha` (suffering) and so on[^262], sorrows with lamentations,

**26.9** displeasure, despairs: this proceeds from `jāti`; \
thus is the coming to be of this whole mass of suffering.

**26.10** The one who does not know constructs the `saṃskāras`, the root of `saṃsāra` (cycle of rebirth)[^263]; hence \
the one who does not know is the agent; therefore the one who knows is not, because of seeing `tattva` (reality).

**26.11** When `avidyā` has ceased, there is no coming to be of the `saṃskāras`; \
but the cessation of `avidyā` is from the development of this very knowledge[^264].

**26.12** By the cessation of each one, each one does not proceed; \
thus this whole mass of suffering completely ceases.

</TabItem>

<TabItem label="Sanskrit (Roman IAST)">
**26.1** punarbhavāya saṃskārānavidyānivṛtastridhā | \
abhisaṃskurute yāṃstairgatiṃ gacchati karmabhiḥ ||

**26.2** vijñānaṃ saṃniviśate saṃskārapratyayaṃ gatau | \
saṃniviṣṭe’tha vijñāne nāmarūpaṃ niṣicyate ||

**26.3** niṣikte nāmarūpe tu ṣaḍāyatanasaṃbhavaḥ | \
ṣaḍāyatanamāgamya saṃsparśaḥ saṃpravartate ||

**26.4** cakṣuḥ pratītya rūpaṃ ca samanvāhārameva ca | \
nāmarūpaṃ pratītyaivaṃ vijñānaṃ saṃpravartate ||

**26.5** saṃnipātastrayāṇāṃ yo rūpavijñānacakṣuṣām | \
sparśaḥ sa tasmāt sparśācca vedanā saṃpravartate ||

**26.6** vedanāpratyayā tṛṣṇā vedanārthaṃ hi tṛṣyate | \
tṛṣyamāṇa upādānamupādatte caturvidham ||

**26.7** upādāne sati bhava upādātuḥ pravartate | \
syāddhi yadyanupādāno mucyeta na bhaved bhavaḥ ||

**26.8** pañca skandhāḥ sa ca bhavo bhavājjātiḥ pravartate | \
jarāmaraṇaduḥkhādi śokāḥ saparidevanāḥ ||

**26.9** daurmanasyamupāyāsā jāteretat pravartate | \
kevalasyaivametasya duḥkhaskandhasya saṃbhavaḥ ||

**26.10** saṃsāramūlaṃ saṃskārānavidvān saṃskarotyataḥ | \
avidvān kārakastasmānna vidvāṃstattvadarśanāt ||

**26.11** avidyāyāṃ niruddhāyāṃ saṃskārāṇāmasaṃbhavaḥ | \
avidyāyā nirodhastu jñānasyāsyaiva bhāvanāt ||

**26.12** tasya tasya nirodhena tat tannābhipravartate | \
duḥkhaskandhaḥ kevalo’yamevaṃ samyag nirudhyate ||

26.1 punarbhavāya saṃskārānavidyānivṛtastridhā abhisaṃskurute yāṃstairgatiṃ gacchati karmabhiḥ

for appearing again | mental constructions | covered by ignorance | in three ways | constructs | which | by those | destination | goes | by actions

and by those `karmans` (actions) one goes to a destination.

one who is craving takes up the fourfold `upādāna` (fuel).

When there is `upādāna`, the `bhava` (existence) of the taker of fuel proceeds;  
for if one were without `upādāna`, one would be released; there would be no `bhava`.

26.8 pañca skandhāḥ sa ca bhavo bhavājjātiḥ pravartate jarāmaraṇaduḥkhādi śokāḥ saparidevanāḥ

And that `bhava` is the five `skandhas` (collections); from `bhava`, `jāti` (birth) proceeds;  
`jarāmaraṇa` (ageing and death), `duḥkha` (suffering) and so on, sorrows with lamentations,

26.9 daurmanasyamupāyāsā jāteretat pravartate kevalasyaivametasya duḥkhaskandhasya saṃbhavaḥ  
🚻⨀①(daurmanasya) 🚹⨂①(upāyāsa) 🚺⨀⑤(jāti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(pravartate) 🚹⨀⑥(kevala) ⏏️(evam) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀⑥(etad) 🚹⨀⑥🆎(duḥkha+skandha) 🚹⨀①(saṃbhava)

displeasure, despairs: this proceeds from `jāti`;  
thus is the coming to be of this whole mass of suffering.

26.10 saṃsāramūlaṃ saṃskārānavidvān saṃskarotyataḥ avidvān kārakastasmānna vidvāṃstattvadarśanāt  
🚻⨀②🆎(saṃsāra+mūla) 🚹⨂②(saṃskāra) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+vidvas) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(saṃskaroti) ⏏️(atas) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+vidvas) 🚹⨀①(kāraka) ⏏️(tasmāt) ⏏️(na) 🚹⨀①(vidvas) 🚻⨀⑤🆎(tattva+darśana)  
the root of the cycle of rebirth | the mental constructions | the one who does not know | constructs | hence | the one who does not know | the agent | therefore | not | the one who knows | because of seeing reality  
The one who does not know constructs the `saṃskāras`, the root of `saṃsāra` (cycle of rebirth); hence  
the one who does not know is the agent; therefore the one who knows is not, because of seeing `tattva` (reality).

26.11 avidyāyāṃ niruddhāyāṃ saṃskārāṇāmasaṃbhavaḥ avidyāyā nirodhastu jñānasyāsyaiva bhāvanāt  
🚺⨀⑦(avidyā) 🚺⨀⑦🔽(ta)(niruddha) 🚹⨂⑥(saṃskāra) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+saṃbhava) 🚺⨀⑥(avidyā) 🚹⨀①(nirodha) ⏏️(tu) 🚻⨀⑥(jñāna) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀⑥(idam) ⏏️(eva) 🚺⨀⑤(bhāvanā)  
ignorance | when … has ceased | of mental constructions | there is no coming to be | of ignorance | cessation | but | of knowledge | of this | very | from the development  
When `avidyā` has ceased, there is no coming to be of the `saṃskāras`;  
but the cessation of `avidyā` is from the development of this very knowledge.

26.12 tasya tasya nirodhena tat tannābhipravartate duḥkhaskandhaḥ kevalo’yamevaṃ samyag nirudhyate

of each one | of each one | by the cessation | each one | each one | not | proceeds | mass of suffering | whole | this | thus | completely | ceases  
By the cessation of each one, each one does not proceed;  
thus this whole mass of suffering completely ceases.

### §27 Dṛṣṭiparīkṣā (Examination of views)

<Tabs syncKey="sanskrit">
<TabItem label="Translation">
**27.1** 'I was in the past time', 'I was not': the `dṛṣṭis` (views) \
which are those[^265], 'eternal world' and so on, rest upon the past[^266].

**27.2** The `dṛṣṭis` 'I shall not be' and 'shall I be another in the future time?'[^267], \
'finite' and so on, rest upon the future.

**27.3** 'I was in the past time' — this is not possible; \
for the one who was in previous births, this one is not that very one.

**27.4** But if it were 'that very one is the self', the `upādāna` (fuel)[^268] differs[^269]; \
freed from `upādāna`, which one, then, is your self?

**27.5** When it is settled that there is no self freed from `upādāna`, \
the self would be the `upādāna` itself; and then again, for you, the self does not exist.

**27.6** And the self is not the `upādāna` itself: that passes away and arises; \
for how indeed will the `upādāna` be the taker of fuel[^270]?

**27.7** And a self other than the `upādāna` is not possible at all; \
for if it were other, it would be apprehended without `upādāna`, and it is not apprehended[^271].

**27.8** Thus it is not other than the `upādāna`, nor is it the `upādāna` itself; \
there is no self without `upādāna`[^272], nor does it not exist: this is certain.

**27.9** 'I was not in the past time' — this is not possible; \
for the one who was in previous births, this one is not other than that.

**27.10** For if this one were other, it would exist even setting that one aside; \
and that one would remain just so[^273], and there it would be born[^274] not having died[^275].

**27.11** Annihilation, the loss of `karman` (actions), and `karman` done by one \
another would experience: this and so on follows.[^276]

**27.12** Nor has it arisen without having been, for here a fault follows[^277]: \
the self would be either made, or else come to be without a primary cause.

**27.13** Thus the `dṛṣṭi` about the past, which is 'I was not', 'I was', \
'both', and 'not both' — this is not possible.

**27.14** The view 'shall I indeed be in the future time?' \
and 'I shall not be' — this is the same as with the past time.

**27.15** If that god is that human[^278], thus it is eternal[^279]; \
and the god would be unarisen, for the eternal is not born[^280].

**27.16** If the human is other than the god[^281], from that it would be non-eternal; \
if the human is other than the god, continuity is not possible[^282].

**27.17** If one part were divine and one part human, \
it would be non-eternal and eternal[^283]; and that is not fitting.

**27.18** If both the non-eternal and the eternal were established, \
that being established, granted, 'neither eternal nor non-eternal' too would be established[^284].

**27.19** If someone, having come from somewhere, were in any way to go again somewhere, \
then that one would be beginningless and eternal[^285]; and that one does not exist.

**27.20** If there is no one eternal, who will be non-eternal, \
both eternal and non-eternal, or set apart from both of these?

**27.21** If the world were finite, how would there be the other world? \
And if the world were infinite, how would there be the other world?

**27.22** Since this continuity[^286] of `skandhas` (collections) is like that of the flames of a lamp[^287], \
therefore neither infinitude nor finitude is fitting.

**27.23** And if the previous ones were to break up, and those `skandhas` \
were not to arise in dependence on these, then the world would be finite.

**27.24** If the previous ones were not to break up, and those `skandhas` \
were not to arise in dependence on these[^288], then the world would be infinite.

**27.25** If one part were finite but one part infinite, \
the world would be finite and infinite; and that is not fitting.

**27.26** How, in the first place, will one part of the taker of fuel perish, \
and one part not perish? And thus this is not fitting.

**27.27** And how indeed will one part of the `upādāna` perish, \
and one part not perish? This too is not possible.

**27.28** If both the finite and the infinite were established, \
that being established, granted, 'neither finite nor infinite' too would be established[^289].

**27.29** Or else, because of the emptiness of all existents, 'eternal' and so on — \
where, of whom, which ones, from what, will the `dṛṣṭis` come to be?

**27.30** The one who, for the abandoning of all `dṛṣṭis`, taught[^290] the true `dharma` (teaching) \
out of compassion — to him, Gautama, I bow.

</TabItem>

<TabItem label="Sanskrit (Roman IAST)">
**27.1** abhūmatītamadhvānaṃ nābhūvamiti dṛṣṭayaḥ | \
yāstāḥ śāśvatalokādyāḥ pūrvāntaṃ samupāśritāḥ ||

**27.2** dṛṣṭayo na bhaviṣyāmi kimanyo’nāgate’dhvani | \
bhaviṣyāmīti cāntādyā aparāntaṃ samāśritāḥ ||

**27.3** abhūmatītamadhvānamityetannopapadyate | \
yo hi janmasu pūrveṣu sa eva na bhavatyayam ||

**27.4** sa evātmeti tu bhavedupādānaṃ viśiṣyate | \
upādānavinirmukta ātmā te katamaḥ punaḥ ||

**27.5** upādānavinirmukto nāstyātmeti kṛte sati | \
syādupādānamevātmā nāsti cātmeti vaḥ punaḥ ||

**27.6** na copādānamevātmā vyeti tat samudeti ca | \
kathaṃ hi nāmopādānamupādātā bhaviṣyati ||

**27.7** anyaḥ punarupādānādātmā naivopapadyate | \
gṛhyeta hyanupādāno yadyanyo na ca gṛhyate ||

**27.8** evaṃ nānya upādānānna copādānameva saḥ | \
ātmā nāstyanupādāno nāpi nāstyeṣa niścayaḥ ||

**27.9** nābhūmatītamadhvānamityetannopapadyate | \
yo hi janmasu pūrveṣu tato’nyo na bhavatyayam ||

**27.10** yadi hyayaṃ bhavedanyaḥ pratyākhyāyāpi taṃ bhavet | \
tathaiva ca sa saṃtiṣṭhet tatra jāyet cāmṛtaḥ ||

**27.11** ucchedaḥ karmaṇāṃ nāśaḥ kṛtamanyena karma ca | \
pratisaṃvedayedanya evamādi prasajyate ||

**27.12** nāpyabhūtvā samudbhūto doṣo hyatra prasajyate | \
kṛtako vā bhavedātmā saṃbhūto vāpyahetukaḥ ||

**27.13** evaṃ dṛṣṭiratīte yā nābhūmahamabhūmaham | \
ubhayaṃ nobhayaṃ ceti naiṣā samupapadyate ||

**27.14** adhvanyanāgate kiṃ nu bhaviṣyāmīti darśanam | \
na bhaviṣyāmi cetyetadatītenādhvanā samam ||

**27.15** sa devaṃ sa manuṣyaścedevaṃ bhavati śāsvatam | \
anutpannaśca devaḥ syājjāyate na hi śāśvatam ||

**27.16** devādanyo manuṣyaścedaśāśvatamato bhavet | \
devādanyo manuṣyaśced saṃtatirnopapadyate ||

**27.17** divyo yadyekadeśaḥ syādekadeśaśca mānuṣaḥ | \
aśāsvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca bhavet tacca na yujyate ||

**27.18** aśāśvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca prasiddhamubhayaṃ yadi | \
siddhe na śāśvataṃ kāmaṃ naivāśāśvatamityapi ||

**27.19** kutaścidāgataḥ kaścit kiṃcid gacchet punaḥ kvacit | \
yadi tasmādanādistu śāśvataḥ syānna cāsti saḥ ||

**27.20** nāsti cecchāśvataḥ kaścit ko bhaviṣyatyaśāśvataḥ | \
śāśvato’śāśvataścāpi dvābhyāmābhyāṃ tiraskṛtaḥ ||

**27.21** antavān yadi lokaḥ syāt paralokaḥ kathaṃ bhavet | \
athāpyanantavāllokaḥ paralokaḥ kathaṃ bhavet ||

**27.22** skandhānāmeṣa saṃtāano yasmād dīpārciṣāmiva | \
tasmānnānantavattvaṃ ca nāntavattvaṃ ca yujyate ||

**27.23** pūrve yadi ca bhajyerannutpadyeranna cāpyamī | \
skandhāḥ skandhān pratītyemānatha loko’ntavān bhavet ||

**27.24** pūrve yadi na bhajyerannutpadyeran na cāpyamī | \
skandhāḥ skandhān pratītyemālloko’nanto bhavedatha ||

**27.25** antavānekadeśaścedekadeśastvanantavān | \
syādantavānanantaśca lokastacca na yujyate ||

**27.26** kathaṃ tāvadupādāturekadeśo vinaṅkṣyate | \
na naṅkṣyate caikadeśa evaṃ caitanna yujyate ||

**27.27** upādānaikadeśaśca kathaṃ nāma vinaṅkṣyate | \
na naṅkṣyate caikadeśo naitadapyupapadyate ||

**27.28** antavaccāpyanantaṃ ca prasiddhamubhayaṃ yadi | \
siddhe naivāntavat kāmaṃ naivānantavadityapi ||

**27.29** atha vā sarvabhāvānāṃ śūnyatvācchāśvatādayaḥ | \
kva kasya katamāḥ kasmāt saṃbhaviṣyanti dṛṣṭayaḥ ||

**27.30** sarvadṛṣṭiprahāṇāya yaḥ saddharmamadeśayet | \
anukampāmupādāya taṃ namasyāmi gautamam ||

<TabItem label="Interlinear">
27.1 abhūmatītamadhvānaṃ nābhūvamiti dṛṣṭayaḥ yāstāḥ śāśvatalokādyāḥ pūrvāntaṃ samupāśritāḥ  
🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) 🚹⨀②🔽(ta)(atīta) 🚹⨀②(adhvan) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) ⏏️(iti) 🚺⨂①(dṛṣṭi) *️⃣🤟🚺⨂①(yad) *️⃣🤟🚺⨂①(tad) 🚺⨂①🆎(śāśvata+loka+ādi) 🚹⨀②(pūrvānta) 🚺⨂①🔽(ta)(samupāśrita)  
I was | in the past | time | not | I was | thus | views | which | those | 'eternal world' and so on | the past | rest upon  
'I was in the past time', 'I was not': the `dṛṣṭis` (views)  
which are those, 'eternal world' and so on, rest upon the past.

27.2 dṛṣṭayo na bhaviṣyāmi kimanyo’nāgate’dhvani bhaviṣyāmīti cāntādyā aparāntaṃ samāśritāḥ  
🚺⨂①(dṛṣṭi) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀👆⏭(bhavati) ⏏️(kim) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(ta)🆎(na+āgata) 🚹⨀⑦(adhvan) 🟢⨀👆⏭(bhavati) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(ca) 🚺⨂①🆎(anta+ādi) 🚹⨀②(aparānta) 🚺⨂①🔽(ta)(samāśrita)  
views | not | I shall be | whether | another | in the future | time | I shall be | thus | and | 'finite' and so on | the future | rest upon  
The `dṛṣṭis` 'I shall not be' and 'shall I be another in the future time?',  
'finite' and so on, rest upon the future.

27.3 abhūmatītamadhvānamityetannopapadyate yo hi janmasu pūrveṣu sa eva na bhavatyayam  
🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) 🚹⨀②🔽(ta)(atīta) 🚹⨀②(adhvan) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upapadyate) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(yad) ⏏️(hi) 🚻⨂⑦(janman) *️⃣🤟🚻⨂⑦(pūrva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) ⏏️(eva) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(idam)  
I was | in the past | time | thus | this | not | is possible | who | for | in births | previous | that | very | not | is | this one  
'I was in the past time' — this is not possible;  
for the one who was in previous births, this one is not that very one.

27.4 sa evātmeti tu bhavedupādānaṃ viśiṣyate upādānavinirmukta ātmā te katamaḥ punaḥ  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(tu) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚻⨀①(upādāna) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(viśiṣyate) 🚹⨀①🆎(upādāna+vinirmukta) 🚹⨀①(ātman) *️⃣🤘⚧⨀⑥(yuṣmad) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(katama) ⏏️(punar)  
that | very | self | thus | but | if it were | fuel | differs | freed from fuel | self | your | which one | then  
But if it were 'that very one is the self', the `upādāna` (fuel) differs;  
freed from `upādāna`, which one, then, is your self?

27.5 upādānavinirmukto nāstyātmeti kṛte sati syādupādānamevātmā nāsti cātmeti vaḥ punaḥ  
🚹⨀①🆎(upādāna+vinirmukta) ⏏️(nāsti) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(iti) 🚻⨀⑦🔽(ta)(kṛta) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(at)(sat) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) 🚻⨀①(upādāna) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(nāsti) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤘⚧⨂④(yuṣmad) ⏏️(punar)  
freed from fuel | there is no | self | thus | when settled | being | would be | fuel | itself | self | does not exist | and | self | thus | for you | then again  
When it is settled that there is no self freed from `upādāna`,  
the self would be the `upādāna` itself; and then again, for you, the self does not exist.

27.6 na copādānamevātmā vyeti tat samudeti ca kathaṃ hi nāmopādānamupādātā bhaviṣyati  
⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(upādāna) ⏏️(eva) 🚹⨀①(ātman) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(vyeti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(samudeti) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(katham) ⏏️(hi) ⏏️(nāma) 🚻⨀①(upādāna) 🚹⨀①(upādātṛ) 🟢⨀🤟⏭(bhavati)  
not | and | fuel | itself | the self | passes away | that | arises | and | how | for | indeed | fuel | the taker of fuel | will be  
And the self is not the `upādāna` itself: that passes away and arises;  
for how indeed will the `upādāna` be the taker of fuel?

27.7 anyaḥ punarupādānādātmā naivopapadyate gṛhyeta hyanupādāno yadyanyo na ca gṛhyate  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) ⏏️(punar) 🚻⨀⑤(upādāna) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upapadyate) 🔴⨀🤟⏯(gṛhyate) ⏏️(hi) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+upādāna) ⏏️(yadi) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(gṛhyate)  
other | and | than fuel | self | not | at all | is possible | would be apprehended | for | without fuel | if | other | not | and | is apprehended  
And a self other than the `upādāna` is not possible at all;  
for if it were other, it would be apprehended without `upādāna`, and it is not apprehended.

27.8 evaṃ nānya upādānānna copādānameva saḥ ātmā nāstyanupādāno nāpi nāstyeṣa niścayaḥ  
⏏️(evam) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🚻⨀⑤(upādāna) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①(upādāna) ⏏️(eva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) 🚹⨀①(ātman) ⏏️(nāsti) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+upādāna) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(api) ⏏️(nāsti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(etad) 🚹⨀①(niścaya)  
thus | not | other | than fuel | nor | and | fuel | itself | it | self | there is no | without fuel | nor | even | does not exist | this | certainty  
Thus it is not other than the `upādāna`, nor is it the `upādāna` itself;  
there is no self without `upādāna`, nor does it not exist: this is certain.

27.9 nābhūmatītamadhvānamityetannopapadyate yo hi janmasu pūrveṣu tato’nyo na bhavatyayam  
⏏️(na) 🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) 🚹⨀②🔽(ta)(atīta) 🚹⨀②(adhvan) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upapadyate) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(yad) ⏏️(hi) 🚻⨂⑦(janman) *️⃣🤟🚻⨂⑦(pūrva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀⑤(tad) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(idam)  
not | I was | in the past | time | thus | this | not | is possible | who | for | in births | previous | than that | other | not | is | this one  
'I was not in the past time' — this is not possible;  
for the one who was in previous births, this one is not other than that.

27.10 yadi hyayaṃ bhavedanyaḥ pratyākhyāyāpi taṃ bhavet tathaiva ca sa saṃtiṣṭhet tatra jāyet cāmṛtaḥ  
⏏️(yadi) ⏏️(hi) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(idam) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🔽(ya)(pratyākhyāti) ⏏️(api) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀②(tad) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) ⏏️(tathā) ⏏️(eva) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(saṃtiṣṭhati) ⏏️(tatra) 🔵⨀🤟⏯(jāyate) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)🆎(na+mṛta)  
if | for | this | were | other | setting aside | even | that one | would exist | so | just | and | that one | would remain | there | would be born | and | not having died  
For if this one were other, it would exist even setting that one aside;  
and that one would remain just so, and there it would be born not having died.

27.11 ucchedaḥ karmaṇāṃ nāśaḥ kṛtamanyena karma ca pratisaṃvedayedanya evamādi prasajyate  
🚹⨀①(uccheda) 🚻⨂⑥(karman) 🚹⨀①(nāśa) 🚻⨀②🔽(ta)(kṛta) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀③(anya) 🚻⨀②(karman) ⏏️(ca) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(pratisaṃvedayati) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🚻⨀①🆎(evam+ādi) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(prasajyate)  
annihilation | of actions | loss | done | by one | action | and | would experience | another | this and so on | follows  
Annihilation, the loss of `karman` (actions), and `karman` done by one  
another would experience: this and so on follows.

27.12 nāpyabhūtvā samudbhūto doṣo hyatra prasajyate kṛtako vā bhavedātmā saṃbhūto vāpyahetukaḥ  
⏏️(na) ⏏️(api) 🔽(tvā)🆎(na+bhavati) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)(samudbhūta) 🚹⨀①(doṣa) ⏏️(hi) ⏏️(atra) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(prasajyate) 🚹⨀①(kṛtaka) ⏏️(vā) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀①(ātman) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)(saṃbhūta) ⏏️(vā) ⏏️(api) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+hetuka)  
not | also | without having been | arisen | a fault | for | here | follows | made | or | would be | the self | come to be | or | else | without a primary cause  
Nor has it arisen without having been, for here a fault follows:  
the self would be either made, or else come to be without a primary cause.

27.13 evaṃ dṛṣṭiratīte yā nābhūmahamabhūmaham ubhayaṃ nobhayaṃ ceti naiṣā samupapadyate  
⏏️(evam) 🚺⨀①(dṛṣṭi) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(ta)(atīta) *️⃣🤟🚺⨀①(yad) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) *️⃣👆⚧⨀①(asmad) 🟢⨀👆⏮(bhavati) *️⃣👆⚧⨀①(asmad) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚺⨀①(etad) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(samupapadyate)  
thus | view | about the past | which | not | I was | I | I was | I | both | not | both | and | thus | not | this | is possible  
Thus the `dṛṣṭi` about the past, which is 'I was not', 'I was',  
'both', and 'not both' — this is not possible.

27.14 adhvanyanāgate kiṃ nu bhaviṣyāmīti darśanam na bhaviṣyāmi cetyetadatītenādhvanā samam  
🚹⨀⑦(adhvan) 🚹⨀⑦🔽(ta)🆎(na+āgata) ⏏️(kim) ⏏️(nu) 🟢⨀👆⏭(bhavati) ⏏️(iti) 🚻⨀①(darśana) ⏏️(na) 🟢⨀👆⏭(bhavati) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(iti) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) 🚹⨀③🔽(ta)(atīta) 🚹⨀③(adhvan) 🚻⨀①(sama)  
in the time | future | whether | indeed | shall I be | thus | view | not | I shall be | and | thus | this | with the past | time | the same  
The view 'shall I indeed be in the future time?'  
and 'I shall not be' — this is the same as with the past time.

27.15 sa devaṃ sa manuṣyaścedevaṃ bhavati śāsvatam anutpannaśca devaḥ syājjāyate na hi śāśvatam  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) 🚹⨀①(deva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad) 🚹⨀①(manuṣya) ⏏️(cet) ⏏️(evam) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(bhavati) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)🆎(na+utpanna) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①(deva) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(jāyate) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(hi) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata)  
that | god | that | human | if | thus | it is | eternal | unarisen | and | god | would be | is born | not | for | the eternal  
If that god is that human, thus it is eternal;  
and the god would be unarisen, for the eternal is not born.

27.16 devādanyo manuṣyaścedaśāśvatamato bhavet devādanyo manuṣyaśced saṃtatirnopapadyate  
🚹⨀⑤(deva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🚹⨀①(manuṣya) ⏏️(cet) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) ⏏️(atas) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) 🚹⨀⑤(deva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(anya) 🚹⨀①(manuṣya) ⏏️(cet) 🚺⨀①(saṃtati) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upapadyate)  
than the god | other | the human | if | non-eternal | from that | would be | than the god | other | the human | if | continuity | not | is possible  
If the human is other than the god, from that it would be non-eternal;  
if the human is other than the god, continuity is not possible.

27.17 divyo yadyekadeśaḥ syādekadeśaśca mānuṣaḥ aśāsvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca bhavet tacca na yujyate  
🚹⨀①(divya) ⏏️(yadi) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①(mānuṣa) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) ⏏️(ca) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
divine | if | one part | were | one part | and | human | non-eternal | eternal | and | would be | that | and | not | is fitting  
If one part were divine and one part human,  
it would be non-eternal and eternal; and that is not fitting.

27.18 aśāśvataṃ śāśvataṃ ca prasiddhamubhayaṃ yadi siddhe na śāśvataṃ kāmaṃ naivāśāśvatamityapi  
🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①🔽(ta)(prasiddha) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(yadi) 🚻⨀⑦🔽(ta)(siddha) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀①(śāśvata) ⏏️(kāmam) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(api)  
the non-eternal | the eternal | and | established | both | if | that being established | neither | eternal | granted | nor | indeed | non-eternal | thus | too  
If both the non-eternal and the eternal were established,  
that being established, granted, 'neither eternal nor non-eternal' too would be established.

27.19 kutaścidāgataḥ kaścit kiṃcid gacchet punaḥ kvacit yadi tasmādanādistu śāśvataḥ syānna cāsti saḥ  
⏏️(kutaścit) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)(āgata) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(kaścit) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀②(kaścit) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(gacchati) ⏏️(punar) ⏏️(kvacit) ⏏️(yadi) ⏏️(tasmāt) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+ādi) ⏏️(tu) 🚹⨀①(śāśvata) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) 🟢⨀🤟▶️(asti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(tad)  
from somewhere | having come | someone | in any way | were to go | again | somewhere | if | then | beginningless | but | eternal | would be | not | and | exists | that one  
If someone, having come from somewhere, were in any way to go again somewhere,  
then that one would be beginningless and eternal; and that one does not exist.

27.20 nāsti cecchāśvataḥ kaścit ko bhaviṣyatyaśāśvataḥ śāśvato’śāśvataścāpi dvābhyāmābhyāṃ tiraskṛtaḥ  
⏏️(nāsti) ⏏️(cet) 🚹⨀①(śāśvata) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(kaścit) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(kim) 🟢⨀🤟⏭(bhavati) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) 🚹⨀①(śāśvata) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+śāśvata) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(api) 🚹⨁③(dvi) *️⃣🤟🚹⨁③(idam) 🚹⨀①🔽(ta)(tiraskṛta)  
there is no | if | eternal | anyone | who | will be | non-eternal | eternal | non-eternal | and | also | from both | of these | set apart  
If there is no one eternal, who will be non-eternal,  
both eternal and non-eternal, or set apart from both of these?

27.21 antavān yadi lokaḥ syāt paralokaḥ kathaṃ bhavet athāpyanantavāllokaḥ paralokaḥ kathaṃ bhavet  
🚹⨀①(antavat) ⏏️(yadi) 🚹⨀①(loka) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) 🚹⨀①🆎(para+loka) ⏏️(katham) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) ⏏️(atha) ⏏️(api) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+antavat) 🚹⨀①(loka) 🚹⨀①🆎(para+loka) ⏏️(katham) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati)  
finite | if | the world | were | the other world | how | would there be | and | also | infinite | the world | the other world | how | would there be  
If the world were finite, how would there be the other world?  
And if the world were infinite, how would there be the other world?

27.22 skandhānāmeṣa saṃtāano yasmād dīpārciṣāmiva tasmānnānantavattvaṃ ca nāntavattvaṃ ca yujyate  
🚹⨂⑥(skandha) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(etad) 🚹⨀①(saṃtāna) ⏏️(yasmāt) 🚻⨂⑥🆎(dīpa+arcis) ⏏️(iva) ⏏️(tasmāt) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀①🔽(tva)🆎(na+antavattva) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) 🚻⨀①🔽(tva)(antavattva) ⏏️(ca) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
of the collections | this | continuity | since | of the flames of a lamp | like | therefore | neither | infinitude | and | nor | finitude | and | is fitting  
Since this continuity of `skandhas` (collections) is like that of the flames of a lamp,  
therefore neither infinitude nor finitude is fitting.

27.23 pūrve yadi ca bhajyerannutpadyeranna cāpyamī skandhāḥ skandhān pratītyemānatha loko’ntavān bhavet  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨂①(pūrva) ⏏️(yadi) ⏏️(ca) 🔵⨂🤟⏯(bhajyate) 🔵⨂🤟⏯(utpadyate) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(api) *️⃣🤟🚹⨂①(adas) 🚹⨂①(skandha) 🚹⨂②(skandha) 🔽(ya)(pratyeti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨂②(idam) ⏏️(atha) 🚹⨀①(loka) 🚹⨀①(antavat) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati)  
the previous ones | if | and | were to break up | were to arise | not | and | also | those | collections | collections | in dependence on | these | then | the world | finite | would be  
And if the previous ones were to break up, and those `skandhas`  
were not to arise in dependence on these, then the world would be finite.

27.24 pūrve yadi na bhajyerannutpadyeran na cāpyamī skandhāḥ skandhān pratītyemālloko’nanto bhavedatha  
*️⃣🤟🚹⨂①(pūrva) ⏏️(yadi) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨂🤟⏯(bhajyate) 🔵⨂🤟⏯(utpadyate) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(api) *️⃣🤟🚹⨂①(adas) 🚹⨂①(skandha) 🚹⨂②(skandha) 🔽(ya)(pratyeti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨂②(idam) 🚹⨀①(loka) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+anta) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(bhavati) ⏏️(atha)  
the previous ones | if | not | were to break up | were to arise | not | and | also | those | collections | collections | in dependence on | these | the world | infinite | would be | then  
If the previous ones were not to break up, and those `skandhas`  
were not to arise in dependence on these, then the world would be infinite.

27.25 antavānekadeśaścedekadeśastvanantavān syādantavānanantaśca lokastacca na yujyate  
🚹⨀①(antavat) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) ⏏️(cet) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) ⏏️(tu) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+antavat) 🟢⨀🤟⏯(asti) 🚹⨀①(antavat) 🚹⨀①🆎(na+anta) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①(loka) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(tad) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
finite | one part | if | one part | but | infinite | were | finite | infinite | and | the world | that | and | not | is fitting  
If one part were finite but one part infinite,  
the world would be finite and infinite; and that is not fitting.

27.26 kathaṃ tāvadupādāturekadeśo vinaṅkṣyate na naṅkṣyate caikadeśa evaṃ caitanna yujyate  
⏏️(katham) ⏏️(tāvat) 🚹⨀⑥(upādātṛ) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) 🔵⨀🤟⏭(vinaśyati) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟⏭(naśyati) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) ⏏️(evam) ⏏️(ca) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(yujyate)  
how | in the first place | of the taker of fuel | one part | will perish | not | will perish | and | one part | thus | and | this | not | is fitting  
How, in the first place, will one part of the taker of fuel perish,  
and one part not perish? And thus this is not fitting.

27.27 upādānaikadeśaśca kathaṃ nāma vinaṅkṣyate na naṅkṣyate caikadeśo naitadapyupapadyate  
🚹⨀①🆎(upādāna+eka+deśa) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(katham) ⏏️(nāma) 🔵⨀🤟⏭(vinaśyati) ⏏️(na) 🔵⨀🤟⏭(naśyati) ⏏️(ca) 🚹⨀①🆎(eka+deśa) ⏏️(na) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(etad) ⏏️(api) 🔵⨀🤟▶️(upapadyate)  
one part of the fuel | and | how | indeed | will perish | not | will perish | and | one part | not | this | too | is possible  
And how indeed will one part of the `upādāna` perish,  
and one part not perish? This too is not possible.

27.28 antavaccāpyanantaṃ ca prasiddhamubhayaṃ yadi siddhe naivāntavat kāmaṃ naivānantavadityapi  
🚻⨀①(antavat) ⏏️(ca) ⏏️(api) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+anta) ⏏️(ca) 🚻⨀①🔽(ta)(prasiddha) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀①(ubhaya) ⏏️(yadi) 🚻⨀⑦🔽(ta)(siddha) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚻⨀①(antavat) ⏏️(kāmam) ⏏️(na) ⏏️(eva) 🚻⨀①🆎(na+antavat) ⏏️(iti) ⏏️(api)  
the finite | and | also | the infinite | and | established | both | if | that being established | neither | indeed | finite | granted | nor | indeed | infinite | thus | too  
If both the finite and the infinite were established,  
that being established, granted, 'neither finite nor infinite' too would be established.

27.29 atha vā sarvabhāvānāṃ śūnyatvācchāśvatādayaḥ kva kasya katamāḥ kasmāt saṃbhaviṣyanti dṛṣṭayaḥ  
⏏️(atha) ⏏️(vā) 🚹⨂⑥🆎(sarva+bhāva) 🚻⨀⑤🔽(tva)(śūnyatva) 🚺⨂①🆎(śāśvata+ādi) ⏏️(kva) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀⑥(kim) *️⃣🤟🚺⨂①(katama) *️⃣🤟🚻⨀⑤(kim) 🟢⨂🤟⏭(saṃbhavati) 🚺⨂①(dṛṣṭi)  
then | or | of all existents | because of emptiness | 'eternal' and so on | where | of whom | which ones | from what | will come to be | views  
Or else, because of the emptiness of all existents, 'eternal' and so on —  
where, of whom, which ones, from what, will the `dṛṣṭis` come to be?

27.30 sarvadṛṣṭiprahāṇāya yaḥ saddharmamadeśayet anukampāmupādāya taṃ namasyāmi gautamam  
🚻⨀④🆎(sarva+dṛṣṭi+prahāṇa) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀①(yad) 🚹⨀②🆎(sad+dharma) 🟢⨀🤟↩️(deśayati) 🚺⨀②(anukampā) 🔽(ya)(upādadāti) *️⃣🤟🚹⨀②(tad) 🟢⨀👆▶️(namasyati) 🚹⨀②(gautama)  
for the abandoning of all views | who | the true teaching | taught | compassion | out of | to him | I bow | Gautama  
The one who, for the abandoning of all `dṛṣṭis`, taught the true `dharma` (teaching)  
out of compassion — to him, Gautama, I bow.

</Tabs>

[^1]: The eight epithets qualify `pratītyasamutpādam` in 0.2, into which the verse runs on, as Garfield (1995) also takes them; Kalupahana (1986) makes them, with `pratītyasamutpāda`, `prapañcopaśama` and `śiva`, ten coordinate objects of "preached" ("the non-ceasing and the non-arising, … the dependent arising, the appeasement of obsessions and the auspicious"), but `aśāśvatam`, `anekārtham` and `anānārtham` can only be adjectives, so all eight are epithets of `pratītyasamutpādam`.
[^2]: The DSBC prints `ya pratītyasamutpādaṃ`; read `yaḥ`, "he who", as Poussin's edition has it.
[^3]: `śivam` is taken with `prapañcopaśamam`, "the auspicious calming of `prapañca`"; Candrakīrti reads it as a further epithet of `pratītyasamutpāda`, "the calming of proliferation, the auspicious", as Kalupahana (1986) also separates it, and the point is the same either way.
[^4]: The DSBC prints `kecanaḥ`; read `kecana`, "of any kind", as Poussin's edition has it.
[^5]: The DSBC prints `heturārambaṇam`, and in 1.8 `anārambaṇa`, `anārambaṇe` and `ārambaṇaṃ`; Poussin's edition reads `hetuś cālambanam` and `anālambana`, `anālambane`, `ālambanaṃ`, and the word is read as `ālambana`, "the objective support", throughout. `ārambaṇa` is also a Buddhist Sanskrit spelling of the word, recorded by Edgerton, so it may be the text's own rather than a slip of the input.
[^6]: `kriyāvantaś ca santy uta`, with `uta` marking the last alternative, asks "and are they with activity?", which after the three denials is answered no, so the pāda is rendered as the fourth denial, as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) also take it; Kalupahana's "nor is it not non-constituted of conditions" for `nāpratyayavatī kriyā` in the first half is a double negative the Sanskrit does not have.
[^7]: `utpadyate` and `notpadyate` are singular and their subject is unexpressed: the fruit, which Candrakīrti supplies. Kalupahana (1986) makes `ime` the subject of both, "depending upon them these [others] arise", and Garfield (1995) "those … come from these"; the plural `ime` of the second half is the subject of `nāpratyayāḥ`, "how are these not non-causes".
[^8]: The DSBC prints `anārambaṇa`, `anārambaṇe` and `ārambaṇaṃ`; read `anālambana`, `anālambane`, `ālambanaṃ`, as in 1.2.
[^9]: Garfield (1995) renders the first half as a condition, "if things did not exist without essence, the phrase … would not be acceptable", from the Tibetan; the Sanskrit `yataḥ` gives the reason, and the second half states what is therefore not possible, as Kalupahana (1986) also reads it.
[^10]: The DSBC prints `bhāvanāṃ`; read `bhāvānāṃ`, "of existents", the genitive plural with which `niḥsvabhāvānāṃ` agrees, as Poussin's edition has it (`bhāvanā`, "cultivation", is impossible here).
[^11]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) render the second half "how can that effect made of conditions [arise] from what is not self-made?"; `yat` closes the relative clause `phalam asvamayebhyo yat`, "that fruit which is from things not made of themselves", and `tat pratyayamayaṃ katham` asks of it how it is made of the causes.
[^12]: `gam` is both "go" and "understand", and `gamyate` "is understood" is a commentator's idiom; Kalupahana (1986) renders the third `gamyate` "present moving is not known" and Garfield (1995) "movement cannot be conceived", with the Tibetan. The one verb "is traversed" is kept for all three, with the verse's three identical `na gamyate` and Candrakīrti's argument, that no third thing besides the gone and the not gone is being traversed.
[^13]: The DSBC's `vigamanaṃ` is read as a `bahuvrīhi`, "without motion", not as "departure"; Poussin's edition reads `hy agamanaṃ`, "for [a being gone] without motion", to the same sense.
[^14]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the last pāda an objection, "[however,] the present moving, indeed, means being moved", and Garfield (1995) "but having motion follows from being a mover", from the Tibetan; `hi` is "for", and the pāda is the ground of the consequence; the adversative is their supplement.
[^15]: `tāvat` on the first limb is "first of all", as Kalupahana (1986) has it in 2.1 ("in the first instance"); his "as much as a mover does not move, a non-mover too does not move" here and in 2.15, and Garfield's (1995) "inasmuch as" here and "just as" in 2.15, make the first denial a reason for, or a comparison with, the second, where the two are coordinate denials.
[^16]: `gantur gamanam icchataḥ` is a genitive participle agreeing with `tasya`: the consequence falls on him who maintains (`iṣ`, "wish, maintain") the goer's motion. Kalupahana (1986) coordinates it with the position, "and who looks for the movement of a mover", and Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, makes it a reason, "because a mover moves".
[^17]: Poussin's edition reads `cocyate`, "is called", and in 2.22–23 `yāyocyate`, as Kalupahana (1986) translates, "is spoken of as a mover"; the DSBC's `cājyate`, `yayājyate` (`añj`, "is made manifest"), which the Tibetan also reflects, is kept, and the sense, the going by which one is shown to be a goer, is the same.
[^18]: The DSBC reads `prāg asti gamanārambhād gamyamānaṃ na vā gatam`, where Poussin's edition has `na pūrvaṃ gamanārambhād`, "not before the beginning of motion is there a being gone, nor a gone", as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate; the DSBC's `na vā` is read as denying both, "neither … nor", to the same sense.
[^19]: `gateḥ samā` is "the same as going", `sama` taking its standard in the genitive, and the subject is the pair `saṃpravṛttiś ca nivṛttiś ca`, the commencing and the ceasing of `gamanam`, motion: the refutation of going (2.3–6) applies to its beginning (2.12–14) and its ending (2.15–17), as Candrakīrti explains it. Kalupahana (1986) keeps motion as a third subject, "movement, commencement and cessation (of movement) are all comparable to motion", and Garfield (1995) "motion and coming to rest and starting to move are similar".
[^20]: The DSBC prints `tayoḥ siddhiṃ`; read `siddhiḥ`, the subject of `vidyate`, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^21]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the last pāda a question, "who or what is it that moves?", and Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "what and where is the thing that moves?"; `kaścit` and `kiṃcit` are indefinites and `hi` is "for": the pāda states the ground, that someone goes some stretch (the `gantavyam` of 2.25), so that there is no goer before a going.
[^22]: The DSBC prints `nopapadyate`; read the dual `nopapadyete`, with its subject `gatī dve`, "two goings", as Poussin's edition has it.
[^23]: Poussin's edition reads the neuter `sadasadbhūtaṃ`, with `gamanam`, which Kalupahana (1986) translates, "a movement, both existent and non-existent"; the DSBC's nominative `sadasadbhūtaḥ`, the goer who is both real and unreal, the third of the goers of 2.24–25, is kept, as Candrakīrti reads it, and Garfield (1995) likewise makes it the mover, "neither an entity nor a non-entity".
[^24]: The DSBC prints `draṣṭavyādini`; read `draṣṭavyādīni`, the neuter plural "what is to be seen and so on", as Poussin's edition has it.
[^25]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) read `yad … tat` as the relative pronoun, "how can that which does not see itself see others?"; the page reads the conjunctions, `yad` "since" with `tat` "then"; both are regular, and the sense is the same.
[^26]: Poussin's edition reads `upagamyatām`, "let it be admitted", as Kalupahana (1986) translates ("one should admit that a seer is explained …"); the DSBC's `avagamyatām`, "let it be understood", is kept, and Garfield (1995), from the Tibetan, also has "is understood".
[^27]: Poussin's edition and the Tibetan have a verse after this one, `pratītya mātāpitarau yathoktaḥ putrasaṃbhavaḥ …`, "as the birth of a son is said to depend on mother and father, so the arising of `vijñāna` is said to depend on the eye and form", Kalupahana's (1986) and Garfield's (1995) 3.7, so that their 3.8 and 3.9 are 3.7 and 3.8 here; the DSBC does not have it, and its numbering is kept.
[^28]: `iti` closes the statement that the four do not exist, and the second half asks what then follows (`bhaviṣyanti punaḥ katham`); Kalupahana (1986) makes the first half a hypothesis, "if it is the view that the four factors … do not exist". The four are `vijñāna`, `sparśa`, `vedanā` and `tṛṣṇā`, the links of `pratītyasamutpāda` before `upādāna`, as Candrakīrti says; Garfield's (1995) "the other four faculties of knowledge" and "all the aggregates, etc." follow the Tibetan, not the Sanskrit.
[^29]: `upādāna` here is the link of `pratītyasamutpāda` that follows `tṛṣṇā`, the grasping that takes up a new birth, Kalupahana (1986) "grasping"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept.
[^30]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) take `darśanenaiva` with `jānīyāt`, "should be known in the same way as seeing"; it is taken here with `vyākhyātam`, "explained … by seeing itself", as 3.5's `vyākhyāto darśanenaiva` has it; either is possible, and the sense is the same.
[^31]: `āhetukam`: `hetu` is here the cause in general, the `kāraṇa` of 4.1–4.6, and Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) write "cause" for both; the glossary's "primary cause", which sets `hetu` apart from `pratyaya` in chapter 1, is kept.
[^32]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) render `arthaḥ` "effect"; the word is "thing, object", as the Tibetan also has it, and the effect, `kārya`, enters only in 4.3.
[^33]: The DSBC prints `satveva`; read `saty eva`, "when form does exist", the counterpart of the second half's `'saty eva`, as Poussin's edition and Candrakīrti's text have it.
[^34]: Garfield (1995) renders "think about form, but do not construct theories about form", Tsongkhapa's gloss; the Sanskrit and the Tibetan forbid every conception about form, and Candrakīrti: `rūpālambanān na kāṃścit parikalpayitum arhati`, "one may not conceive any [conceptions] that have form as their object". Kalupahana (1986), "one should not discriminatively think of anything confined to material form", detaches `kāṃścit` as a separate object; `rūpagatān kāṃścin … vikalpān` are three accusatives in agreement, "any conceptions about form".
[^35]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `sadṛśam` "identical with the cause", the Sāṃkhya thesis of the effect's identity with its cause; the word is "like, similar", as Garfield (1995) has it, and Candrakīrti glosses the pair as similarity, `kāryakāraṇayoḥ sādṛśyam eva nāsti`.
[^36]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "all existents such as feeling, thought, perception and dispositions", making the four the whole; `sarveṣām eva bhāvānām` extends the procedure beyond the four named (the Tibetan: "and all existents too"), and Candrakīrti: `yathaiva hy ekasya dharmasya śūnyatā … tathaiva sarvadharmāṇām api`, "as the emptiness of one dharma, so of all dharmas".
[^37]: `śūnyatayā` goes with `kṛte`, not with `vadet`: the objection is the one made by means of emptiness, and the rebuttal is the opponent's, as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) construe it with the Tibetan; Candrakīrti: `śūnyatayā kāraṇabhūtayā … sasvabhāvavāde pratiṣiddhe, yadi paraḥ parihāraṃ brūyāt`, "when the doctrine of intrinsic nature has been refuted with emptiness as the ground, if the opponent should state a rebuttal".
[^38]: As in 4.8, `śūnyatayā` goes with `kṛte`: the explanation is the one made by means of emptiness and the criticism the objector's; Candrakīrti: `vyākhyānakāle 'pi yaḥ … upālambhaṃ kuryāt, tasyāpi tac codyam … pūrvavat sādhyasamaṃ veditavyam`, "whoever, at the time of the explanation too, should raise a criticism, that objection of theirs likewise is to be known, as before, as equal to what is to be proved".
[^39]: The DSBC prints `prasajyet`; read `prasajyeta`, "it would follow", as Poussin's edition and Candrakīrti's text have it: the pāda `alakṣaṇaṃ prasajyeta` is eight syllables where `alakṣaṇaṃ prasajyet` would be seven.
[^40]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "nor does it proceed from something other than those with or without characteristic", giving the ablative to `pravartate`; it goes with `anyatra`, "elsewhere than", a third locus, as Garfield (1995) has it, and Candrakīrti answers the objection that the characteristic might apply elsewhere than to the two (`salakṣaṇālakṣaṇābhyām anyatra pravartiṣyata iti`) with `asadbhāvāt`, there being no such third thing.
[^41]: `kasyābhāvo bhaviṣyati` asks "of what will there be non-existence?", `abhāva` being here the non-existence of something, Kalupahana (1986) "whose non-existence", Garfield (1995) "of what will there be nonexistence?"; the glossary's noun "non-existent" is kept.
[^42]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "who could comprehend the distinct things: existent and non-existent as well as existence and non-existence?", making `bhāvābhāvavidharmā` an object; it is nominative singular, agreeing with `kaḥ`, and describes the knower, as Garfield (1995) has it; Candrakīrti: `na ca bhāvābhāvavisadṛśadharmā kaścit tṛtīyaḥ padārtho 'sti, yo 'nayor avagamakaḥ`, "nor is there any third thing, of a nature unlike the existent and the non-existent, that would be the knower of the two".
[^43]: Garfield (1995) has "not without character" for `nāpi lakṣaṇam`; the Sanskrit and the Tibetan alike say "nor a characteristic", the four limbs being existent, non-existent, characterised and characteristic.
[^44]: Kalupahana (1986) makes space a fifth item negated, "neither space nor the other five elements similar to space"; `ākāśam` is the subject of the four negations and `ākāśasamā dhātavaḥ pañca ye 'pare` a statement, as Garfield (1995) has it; Candrakīrti: `yathā cākāśam, evam ākāśasamā dhātavaḥ pañca ye pare`, "and as space, so the five other components are the same as space".
[^45]: Garfield (1995) has "Fools and reificationists" for `alpabuddhayaḥ`, "those of little intelligence" (so the Tibetan), and no rendering of `śivam`, "auspicious"; "reificationists" is Tsongkhapa's gloss.
[^46]: Garfield (1995) renders "whether or not desire or the desirous one exist, the analysis would be the same", folding `rakte 'pi` into the alternative; it is a locative of reference, "for the impassioned too", the procedure applied to passion in 6.1–6.2 being applied to the impassioned in turn, as Kalupahana (1986) and the Tibetan read it; Candrakīrti: `eṣa eva rāgānupapattikramo 'nantarokto rakte 'pi tulyaḥ`, "this very procedure of passion's impossibility, just stated, holds equally for the impassioned too".
[^47]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `na tenaiva hi tat saha` "that which is associated does not arise together"; the words say that a thing is not together with its very self, as Garfield (1995) and the Tibetan have it, and Candrakīrti: `na hi rāgasvātmā rāgād avyatirikto rāgeṇa saheti vyapadiśyate`, "for passion's own self, not distinct from passion, is not called 'together with passion'".
[^48]: The DSBC prints `pṛthakpṛthagbhāgaḥ`, "a portion [of each]"; read `pṛthakpṛthagbhāvaḥ`, "a separate existence of each", the word of 6.7 and 6.9 (`pṛthakpṛthagbhāvo`, `pṛthagbhāva`), which Poussin's edition, the Tibetan and Candrakīrti read (`kim anayoḥ siddhaḥ pṛthakpṛthagbhāvaḥ? kiṃ rāganirapekṣo raktaḥ siddho yatas tayoḥ sahabhāvaḥ syāt`). Poussin's edition also reads `yatas` for the DSBC's `tatas`, "from which" for "and hence", as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate it; the sense is the same and the DSBC is kept.
[^49]: Garfield (1995) renders the second half "since they are established as simultaneous, do you also assert that they are different?"; `sahabhāvaprasiddhyartham` is purpose, "for the sake of establishing coexistence" (so the Tibetan), as Kalupahana (1986) has it, and Candrakīrti draws out the circle: `nanv evaṃ sati itaretarāśrayāyāṃ siddhau sthitāyāṃ kasyedānīṃ siddhau satyāṃ kasya siddhir astu`, "but then, the establishment resting on each other, which is to be established first, for the other to be established?".
[^50]: Garfield (1995) makes `sahabhāvo na sidhyati` a condition, "if one is asserting simultaneity"; it is a statement, "coexistence is not established", in the Sanskrit and the Tibetan alike, as Kalupahana (1986) has it.
[^51]: `saha` and `asaha` are, in Candrakīrti's gloss, establishment in coexistence and in sequence: `rāgaraktayor na paurvāparyeṇa siddhiḥ nāpi sahabhāvena`, "passion and the impassioned are established neither one after the other nor in coexistence"; Kalupahana (1986) "with or without the lustful one", Garfield (1995) "as simultaneous or not simultaneous".
[^52]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the second half "how can there be characteristics of the conditioned?" and Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "How could the characteristics of the produced exist?", making the characteristic the subject; the Sanskrit has two nominatives, `utpādaḥ` and `saṃskṛtalakṣaṇam`, predicated of each other, and asks how an unconstructed arising can be a characteristic of the `saṃskṛta`.
[^53]: Kalupahana (1986) takes the genitive `utpādasthitibhaṅgānām` with `anyat`, "a characteristic of the conditioned other than arising, duration, and destruction", a fourth characteristic; it is possessive, a further characteristic of the three themselves, since `te na saṃskṛtāḥ` (masculine plural) says that the three would then not be `saṃskṛta`, and 7.4 answers with the arising of arising. Garfield (1995) reads as the page.
[^54]: The DSBC prints `mulotpādasya`; read `mūlotpādasya`, as in 7.5 and in Poussin's edition.
[^55]: Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, has the arising of arising "give rise to the basic arising" and "the arising of the basic arising give rise to arising"; in the Sanskrit the first half has no verb of producing, the arising of arising being the arising of the root arising alone, and in the second `maulo utpādaḥ` is the nominative and `utpādotpādam` the accusative: the root arising generates the arising of arising, as Kalupahana (1986) has it.
[^56]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the first half "If arising of arising is the primary arising", dropping the genitive `mūlotpādasya`; the arising of arising is the arising of the root arising, not identical with it, and the question of the second half turns on the two being distinct. Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, has the arising of arising "give rise to the basic arising", as in his 7.4.
[^57]: Kalupahana (1986) reads `kāmam` as "if it may so desire" and the second half as a consequence, "so that it, being not yet born, will be able to produce that"; `kāmam` is the adverb "granted, readily", conceding the first half, and `yadi … śaknuyāt` is the condition on which it is conceded. Garfield (1995) makes what is arising the object, "It will give rise to that which is arising"; `ayam utpadyamānaḥ` is the nominative subject and `imam` the object.
[^58]: The DSBC prints `casau`; read `cāsau`, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^59]: The DSBC prints the accusative `dīpaṃ`, which would make the lamp the object of `prakāśayate`, "what illuminates the lamp?"; Poussin's edition reads the nominative `dīpaḥ`, and the lamp is read as the subject, "what does the lamp illuminate?", which the argument of 7.10–7.12 requires and which Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) also translate.
[^60]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `pratītyotpattim` as the compound "dependent arising" ("spoken of as dependent arising") and `utpattau` as "from within arising"; `pratītya` is the gerund and the accusative `utpattim` its object (a predicate of `ucyate` would stand in the nominative), "arising in dependence on origination", which 7.16 takes up with `pratītya yad yad bhavati`, and the locative `utpattau` is the goal of `kramate`, "proceed to". Garfield (1995) reads as the page.
[^61]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `śāntaṃ svabhāvataḥ` "inherently peaceful" and Garfield (1995) "essentially peaceful", taking the suffix `-taḥ` as "by nature"; it can equally mean "as regards", and the page reads "at peace in respect of `svabhāva`", at peace as to intrinsic nature, that is, without one, which is the chapter's argument against arising by intrinsic nature. Both readings are possible.
[^62]: `tasmin bhāva utpadyate 'sati` resolves either as the locative `bhāve`, in the absolute `tasmin … bhāve … asati`, "when that existent does not exist, what arises?", which the page reads and Garfield (1995) has ("But if it were nonexistent, what could arise?"), or as the nominative `bhāvaḥ`, "when that does not exist, what existent arises?", which Kalupahana (1986) renders "how can an existent arise?"; both are possible.
[^63]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "If arising were to produce this present arising", taking `ayam` with `utpadyamānam`; `ayam` is masculine nominative and goes with `utpādaḥ`, "this arising", the neuter `utpadyamānam` being its object, as Garfield (1995) has it.
[^64]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "If this arising were to produce another"; `anyaḥ … utpādaḥ` is the nominative and `enam` the accusative: the other arising produces this one, as Garfield (1995) has it.
[^65]: Poussin's edition reads the indicative `sarvam utpadyate tathā`, "everything arises in that way", which Garfield (1995) follows ("all things could arise in this way"); the DSBC's imperative `utpadyatāṃ` is kept. Kalupahana (1986), "then it will produce everything in this manner", makes the intransitive verb transitive on either reading.
[^66]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `anirudhyamānaḥ` "non-arising", a slip for "not ceasing", as he has it in 7.23, where the line recurs.
[^67]: Poussin's edition has the first two clauses in the other order, `na sthitabhāvas tiṣṭhaty asthitabhāvo na tiṣṭhati`, which Kalupahana (1986) follows ("An existent that has endured is not stationary, nor is an existent that has not endured"); the DSBC's order is kept.
[^68]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) read the locative `jarāmaraṇadharmeṣu sarvabhāveṣu` as absolute, "When all existents are always of the nature of decay and death" and "Inasmuch as the nature of all things is aging and death"; the page reads it as partitive with `katame bhāvāḥ`, "among all existents … which existents". Both are possible and the point is the same.
[^69]: The DSBC prints `nirūddhaṃ` twice; read `niruddhaṃ`, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^70]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `kim` as the question particle, "Is it the unborn that ceases?"; it is the pronoun, "what, unborn, ceases?", on the pattern of 7.22 `ko 'nutpannaś ca tiṣṭhati`, where `kaḥ` can only be the pronoun, and as Garfield (1995) has it.
[^71]: The DSBC prints `ananyāvasthayāvasthā`, "by a state that is not other"; read `anyayāvasthayāvasthā`, "nor does a state, being another, cease by another state", as Poussin's edition has it and as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate: the second half denies cessation by another state as the first denies it by the very same one.
[^72]: Poussin's edition reads `tadaiva`, "just then", answering `yadaiva`, which Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate ("then"); the DSBC's `tadaivaṃ`, "then, in this way", is kept.
[^73]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `īhate` impersonally, "Neither is it intended that a really non-existent agent performs a really non-existent action"; the nominative `kārakaḥ … asadbhūtaḥ` is its subject and `īh` is "aim at, attempt": the unreal agent does not attempt the unreal action. Garfield (1995) has "perform", with the Tibetan.
[^74]: The DSBC prints `kāraṇaṃ` in the second half as in the first; Poussin's edition reads `karaṇaṃ`, the instrument, which completes the triad activity, agent, instrument, and the word is rendered so (`kāraṇa` itself has the sense "instrument, means"), the cause having just been denied. Kalupahana (1986) renders it "performance of action", the other sense of `karaṇa`; Garfield (1995) "action", with the Tibetan.
[^75]: `dharma` here is merit and `adharma` demerit, the pair that bears a fruit and, in 8.6, makes a path to release and heaven possible, Kalupahana (1986) "good and bad"; Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, renders `dharmādharmau` "Entities and nonentities". The glossary's senses, phenomenon and teaching, do not apply, and the word is glossed "merit" here.
[^76]: The DSBC prints `dharmādharmo`; read the dual `dharmādharmau`, the subject of the dual `vidyete`, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^77]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `na san nāsac ca` as one object, "an action that exists and does not exist", and Garfield (1995) has "an action that is unreal or both real and unreal", the pattern of his 8.9–8.10; the two negatives give two objects, neither a real nor an unreal `karman`, which with 8.9–8.10 completes the nine pairings of the three agents with the three actions.
[^78]: `upādāna` here is what is taken up, with its taker, the pair that chapter 9 examines, Kalupahana (1986) "grasping", Garfield (1995) "appropriation"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept.
[^79]: Garfield (1995) makes the first half a ground, "Since sight and hearing, etc., and feeling, etc., exist", and supplies "he who has and uses them", the Tibetan's gloss of the possessor; the Sanskrit has only the relative clause `bhavanti yasya`, "whose they are" (Candrakīrti: `yasya upādātuḥ`, "of which taker of fuel"), which Kalupahana (1986) keeps, "For whomsoever there exists seeing, hearing, etc."; nothing in `yasya` is causal.
[^80]: The DSBC's chapter heading prints `upadātrupādānaparīkṣā`; read `upādātṛ-`, "the taker of fuel", the one who takes up seeing, hearing, feeling and the rest as his `upādāna` (Candrakīrti, of the opponent's view: `sa darśanādikam upādānam upādatte`), the word rendered "fuel" after chapter 10's simile. Poussin's edition names the chapter `pūrvaparīkṣā`, Kalupahana (1986) "Examination of the Prior Entity"; the DSBC's title is kept.
[^81]: The DSBC prints `kiṃcit kiṃcid vinā kutaḥ`; read `kiṃcit kaṃcid vinā`, "whence something without someone?", the counterpart of `kiṃcid vinā kaścit` in the first half, as Poussin's edition reads it, as Candrakīrti glosses it (`kenacic cātmanā kiṃcid upādānam … abhivyajyate`, "and by some self some fuel is made manifest") and as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate it.
[^82]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `darśanādīnām anyena … anyadā` "by things different from seeing, etc., on different occasions"; `anya` "other than" takes the ablative, and the genitive is partitive, "by another one of seeing and the rest at another time", the opponent's rejoinder that 9.7 answers with `ekaikasmāt`: Candrakīrti introduces it with `kiṃ tarhi ekaikasmāt pūrvo vidyate`, "rather, he is found prior to each one", and explains that when he is made manifest as seer by seeing he is not then designated by hearing and the rest. Garfield (1995) "Through seeing, etc., by another one, that other one becomes disclosed" is the same construal.
[^83]: Garfield (1995) renders "that from which these are arisen: there is no existent there", the Tibetan's word for the elements also meaning "arising"; the Sanskrit has `eṣa`, "this one", the self, as the subject and `teṣu … bhūteṣu`, "in those elements", as the place (Candrakīrti: `yebhyo mahābhūtebhyo darśanādikam utpadyate, teṣv api … eṣa na yujyate`, "in those great elements too from which seeing and the rest arise, this one is not fitting"), as Kalupahana (1986) reads; `bhūta` is the four material elements, not the `dhātu` (component) of chapter 5.
[^84]: Garfield (1995) renders "For whomever … there is nothing, for such a one … conceptions will cease", the Tibetan's dative subject and future verb; in the Sanskrit the relative `yaḥ … na vidyate` has the self as the subject of "is not found", `tatra` is "with regard to him" (Candrakīrti: `tasya … astitvaṃ nāstitvaṃ vā kaḥ parikalpayet prājñaḥ`, "what wise person would conceive existence or non-existence of him?") and `nivṛttāḥ` is a past participle, "have ceased", as Kalupahana (1986) reads.
[^85]: `karman` here is the grammatical object, the thing burnt, the pair `kartṛ`–`karman` being agent and object: Candrakīrti glosses `tasya dagdhā kartā agniḥ`, "the fire is its burner, the agent", and compares pot and potter, cutter and what is cut, and 10.2's `akarmakaḥ` is the grammarians' "without an object". Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) render "agent and action", the glossary's other sense of `karman` (action), which is why the gloss here reads "(object)".
[^86]: `punarārambha-` is read as the compound, "beginning again", the re-kindling of a fire that is always alight; Kalupahana (1986) takes `punar` as the particle, "Furthermore, its commencement will be rendered meaningless", and Garfield (1995) omits it. Candrakīrti repeats `punarārambhavaiyarthyaṃ ca syāt` as one of the fire's three consequences and glosses the pointless undertaking as its fuelling and fanning up to keep it from going out (`upādānasaṃdhukṣaṇādikaṃ vyartham eva syāt`), which favours the compound; the particle reading, "furthermore, beginning is pointless", is equally grammatical. He attaches `evaṃ ca sati`, "and, this being so", to `akarmakaḥ` alone, as Kalupahana and Garfield do.
[^87]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the fire the subject, "would imply the meaninglessness of its commencement", and Garfield (1995) makes `nityadīptaḥ` a condition, "If it were eternally in flames", with the Tibetan; `prasajyate` is intransitive, "it follows", and `nityadīptaḥ` (nominative, as `nityapradīpta eva syāt` in 10.2) and `-vaiyarthyam` are the two things that follow, as Candrakīrti spells them out (`nityapradīpta eva bhavet … vyartham eva syāt`).
[^88]: Poussin's edition reads `tatraitasmāt`, "herein, from this", whence Kalupahana (1986) "Herein, if it is assumed … and, therefore"; the DSBC's `tatraitat syāt`, "if here this should be so", is kept. The opponent's rejoinder (`iti cet`) either way, as Candrakīrti takes it (`yadi manyase`, "if you think").
[^89]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the fuel the subject and the verbs passive, "[Fuel] that is different is not reached; the unreached is not ignited", and adds a simile, "like one that has its own mark"; Garfield (1995) has "they" and "connected". `anyaḥ` is masculine, the fire (`indhana` is neuter), `prāpsyate` "will reach", `dhakṣyati` "will burn", `anirvāṇaḥ` "not gone out", and there is no "like" in the Sanskrit: Candrakīrti, `so 'nyatvād … indhanaṃ na prāpnuyāt … anirvāṇaś ca svaliṅgavān eva sthāsyati, pradīpta ity arthaḥ`, "being other, it would not reach the firewood … and not gone out it will remain bearing its own mark, that is, ablaze".
[^90]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "which of them is pre-accomplished so that fire could be contingent upon fuel?", taking `indhanam` as the object of `apekṣya`; `indhanam` beside the nominative `agniḥ` is the second thing established, as Candrakīrti puts the question, `kim indhanaṃ yad apekṣyāgniḥ syāt, uta agnir yam apekṣyendhanaṃ syāt`, "is it the firewood, depending on which there would be fire, or the fire, depending on which there would be firewood?"; Garfield's (1995) two questions are the Tibetan's.
[^91]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) render "Fire is not dependent upon fuel; fire is not independent of fuel", turning the absolutive into a predicate; the Sanskrit negates the fire, `apekṣyendhanam agnir na`, "there is no fire depending on the firewood", the absolutive qualifying the noun, so that the verse denies a fire of either kind.
[^92]: Garfield (1995) renders "fire and the rest are just like the moved, the not-moved, and the goer", with the Tibetan; the Sanskrit has `atrendhane śeṣam uktam`, "here, as to the firewood, the rest has been stated", by chapter 2's analysis with the words changed, as Candrakīrti writes it out, `dagdhaṃ na dahyate tāvad adagdhaṃ naiva dahyate`, "the burnt is not being burnt, nor is the unburnt being burnt", and as Kalupahana (1986) reads.
[^93]: Garfield (1995) renders `nāgnir anyatra cendhanāt` "Fire does not arise from anything different from fuel"; the Sanskrit has no verb of arising, and `anyatra … indhanāt`, "elsewhere than the firewood", is the second of the five positions, otherness (Candrakīrti: `anyatvasya pratiṣedhāt`, "by the denial of otherness"), origin having been denied in 10.13; Kalupahana (1986) "Apart from fuel there is no fire".
[^94]: `upādāna` here is what the self takes up, the five collections: Candrakīrti, `upādīyate ity upādānaṃ pañcopādānaskandhāḥ`, "the `upādāna` is what is taken up, the five `upādānaskandhas`", and he who is designated on the basis of them is the `upādātṛ`, the self; Kalupahana (1986) "grasping", Garfield (1995) "the aggregates". The glossary's "fuel", from this chapter's simile, is kept, and `indhana` is "firewood" to keep the two apart.
