[^95]: Kalupahana (1986) attaches `sārdhaṃ ghaṭapaṭādibhiḥ` to the means, "through the examples of fire and fuel, together with the examples of pot, cloth"; the instrument is `agnīndhanābhyām` alone, and the pot and the cloth are further objects of the same fivefold analysis, each against its cause, its parts, its characteristic and its qualities, as Candrakīrti says (`sa nānayor eva, kiṃ tarhi sārdhaṃ ghaṭapaṭādibhiḥ`, "it is not of these two alone, but together with pot, cloth and the rest") and as Garfield (1995) reads.
[^96]: `satattvam … pṛthak pṛthak`: Candrakīrti glosses `satattvam` as `apṛthaktvam, ananyatvam, ekatvam`, "non-separateness, non-otherness, oneness", and `pṛthak pṛthak` as the declaring of otherness, so that the verse rejects both the sameness and the difference of the self and existents that the chapter has examined; so Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "the self is the same as or different from the entities". Kalupahana (1986) takes `satattva` in its dictionary sense, "having reality": "the substantiality of the self as well as of discrete existents".
[^97]: Poussin's edition names the chapter `pūrvāparakoṭiparīkṣā`, Kalupahana (1986) "Examination of the Prior and Posterior Extremities"; the DSBC's `saṃsāraparīkṣā` is kept.
[^98]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `amṛtaḥ` "an immortal" and Garfield (1995) "a deathless one"; the word is both (MW "immortal"; `a-mṛta`, "not dead"), and Candrakīrti takes it of one who has not died elsewhere, in a former life, `anyatrāmṛtasya ihotpādo nāsti`, "there is no arising here of one who has not died elsewhere", the counterpart of 11.4's `jarāmaraṇam ajātasya`, "ageing and death of one unborn".
[^99]: Poussin's edition reads `na jarāmaraṇenaiva jātiś ca saha yujyate`, "nor is `jāti` fitting together with `jarāmaraṇa`", whence Kalupahana (1986) "decay-death as concomitant of birth"; the DSBC's two nominatives, `jarāmaraṇaṃ caiva jātiś ca`, say the same and are kept.
[^100]: Garfield (1995) renders "Why are you led to posit", the Tibetan's second person, and Kalupahana (1986) "why be obsessed by"; `prapañcayanti` is third plural, "they", those who hold the sequences (Candrakīrti reads it of the fools, `avastuka eva bālānāṃ prapañcaḥ`, "the fools' proliferation has no real object", or, with `kim` as "not at all", of the liberated ones, who do not), and it is the verb of the glossary's `prapañca` (proliferation): to spread out, elaborate, falsely imagine (Edgerton, of the same verb at 22.15).
[^101]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `santy arthā ye ca kecana` "or whatever fruits there are"; `artha` is "thing", Candrakīrti's `padārthāḥ`, knowledge and the known, proof and the proved, part and whole, quality and its bearer, the list that 11.8 resumes as "all existents".
[^102]: The DSBC prints `bhāvanāṃ`; read `bhāvānāṃ`, "of all existents", the genitive plural with `sarveṣām`, as Poussin's edition has it; its `koṭī` for `koṭiḥ` in the same line is a by-form of the word (MW) and stands.
[^103]: `tac ca kāryaṃ na yujyate`: `kārya` is read as the gerundive, "to be made", with Candrakīrti's gloss `tad duḥkham iṣyamāṇaṃ kāryaṃ kartavyaṃ na yujyate` and Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "It is not the kind of thing to be produced"; Kalupahana (1986) reads the noun, "[Suffering as] such an effect is indeed not appropriate", as 11.7 uses `kārya` for the effect. Both are grammatical and make the same point, that suffering produced in any of the four ways is not fitting.
[^104]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "If from these those that are different were to come to be", taking the ablatives as origin and supplying "come to be"; `anya` with the ablative is "other than" (Candrakīrti: `anye syuḥ`, "would be other", of the collections of the birth and of the death). In the last clause both he and Garfield (1995) swap the demonstratives, "these are caused by those"; the Sanskrit has `parair ebhir amī kṛtāḥ`, "those made by these others", as Candrakīrti paraphrases it, `etaiḥ parabhūtaiḥ skandhair amī parabhūtāḥ kṛtāḥ`.
[^105]: Kalupahana (1986) splits the verse into a statement, "that own person can exist without suffering", and a question; the Sanskrit is one question, `duḥkhaṃ punar vinā svapudgalaḥ sa katamaḥ`, "which is that own person apart from suffering?", and "can exist" is supplied; Garfield (1995) reads as the page does.
[^106]: Garfield (1995) asks about the giver, "who is that person — when suffering is given by another"; the Sanskrit's `yasmai pradīyate … sa duḥkhena vinā kutaḥ` asks about the recipient, "he to whom it is given — whence is he apart from suffering?", the giver being 12.6's question: Candrakīrti closes 12.5 with `pratigrāhaka eva nāsti`, "there is no recipient at all", and opens 12.6 with `yaś ca dadāti, asāv api nāsti`, "and the one who gives, he too does not exist"; Kalupahana (1986) "the latter" as the page.
[^107]: The DSBC prints this verse in square brackets, opened before it and closed before 12.7; Poussin's edition has it without brackets, Candrakīrti comments on it, and Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate it, so it is translated like the rest.
[^108]: The DSBC prints `yad kuryāt`, unsandhied; read `yat kuryāt`, as Poussin's edition has it. Its `svayaṃ kṛtasya` with a space, at the start of the verse, is the compound it writes `svayaṃkṛtam` at the end.
[^109]: Garfield (1995) renders "Whoever caused the suffering of another must have caused his own suffering", with the Tibetan; the Sanskrit's `paro … duḥkhaṃ yat kuryāt` has the other as the agent of "would make", and `tat tasya syāt svayaṃkṛtam` "that would be self-made for him" (Candrakīrti: `manuṣyapudgalasya svayaṃkṛtam eva tad bhavati`, "for the human person it is simply self-made"), as Kalupahana (1986) reads.
[^110]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "So long as suffering is not self-caused, it is indeed not caused by oneself" and "If the other were not to do it by himself"; `tāvat` here is "first, to begin with" ("so long as" wants `yāvat`), `tenaiva tat kṛtam` is "that is made by that very [suffering]", the referent being suffering itself, which cannot act on itself (Candrakīrti: `svātmani vṛttivirodhāt`, "because of the contradiction of operating on oneself"), and `paro nātmakṛtaḥ` is a bahuvrīhi of the other, "the other is not made by itself" (Candrakīrti: `sa eva tāvan nātmanā kṛtaḥ`), as Garfield (1995) reads.
[^111]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "If suffering were to be caused by both, it would be caused by each individually", reversing condition and consequence; `yadi` closes the second clause, `syād ekaikakṛtaṃ yadi`, so "it would be made by both, if it could be made by each one", which 12.2–12.8 has refuted, as Candrakīrti has it, `yadi hi ekaikena duḥkhasya karaṇaṃ syāt, syāt tadānīm ubhābhyāṃ kṛtaṃ duḥkham`, and as Garfield (1995) reads.
[^112]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `bāhyānām … bhāvānām` "other existents"; `bāhya` is "external, outside", the things outside the person, Candrakīrti `bāhyā api bījāṅkuraghaṭapaṭādayo bhāvāḥ`, "external existents too, seed, sprout, pot, cloth and the rest", as Garfield (1995) reads.
[^113]: `saṃskāras` here are the constructed things, all of which are of deceptive quality, Kalupahana (1986) "dispositions", Garfield (1995) "compounded phenomena"; the glossary's "mental constructions" is kept.
[^114]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the second half "All things that are of deceptive nature involve dispositions", reversing subject and predicate (his note: not all dharmas delude, only the `saṃskāras`); in `sarve ca moṣadharmāṇaḥ saṃskārāḥ` the `saṃskāras` are the subject and `moṣadharmāṇaḥ` the predicate, as the syllogism requires (what is of deceptive quality is false; all `saṃskāras` are of deceptive quality; therefore they are false) and as Garfield (1995) reads it.
[^115]: Kalupahana (1986) reads `tu` as "too" and the compound in apposition, "That too, namely, that which illuminates emptiness, has been spoken of by the Blessed One"; Garfield (1995) "What deceives?" and "has said about this that emptiness is completely true", a paraphrase of the Tibetan. `tu`, "but", marks the reply to the objection, `muṣyate` is passive, "what is deceived (literally robbed, √muṣ, the root of `moṣa`) there?", and `śūnyatāparidīpakam` is the predicate of `etat`, "spoken … as illuminating `śūnyatā`".
[^116]: The DSBC reads `kasyātha`, a question, "or else, of what other than milk will there be the state of curd?"; Poussin's edition reads `kasyacid`, whence Kalupahana (1986) "Butter-ness would then be something other than milk". The DSBC is kept; Garfield's (1995) "Or curd would have come to be an entity different from milk" shows that the Tibetan had `atha` too.
[^117]: Poussin's edition reads `syāc chūnyam iti kiṃcana`, whence Kalupahana (1986) "there would then be something called empty"; the DSBC's `śūnyam api`, "something empty too", is kept, the point, that the empty is relative to the not empty, being the same either way.
[^118]: Garfield (1995) renders `tān asādhyān babhāṣire` "That one will accomplish nothing", reading the Tibetan's passive actively; `asādhya` is the gerundive, "not to be mastered, incurable" (Monier-Williams), agreeing with `tān`, the persons, as Kalupahana (1986) "incorrigible" has it, and the Kāśyapaparivarta that Candrakīrti quotes here says of one for whom `śūnyatā` is itself a view `tam aham acikitsyam iti vadāmi`, "him I call incurable", with its simile of the medicine that purges the disease and then stays in the body.
[^119]: Garfield (1995) attaches "in this threefold way" to the whole verse; `evam` carries the triad of 14.1 over to passion, and `traidhena` opens the second half for the remaining `kleśas` and `āyatanas`, as Kalupahana (1986) "should be seen in the triadic mode" has it; the sense is the same.
[^120]: The DSBC prints `na vidhate`; read `na vidyate`, "is not found", as Poussin's edition prints it and 14.4 repeats it: the cadence of the pāda wants its sixth syllable, `vid`, heavy.
[^121]: Garfield (1995) reads `sārdham` as a time, "When one thing and another are simultaneous, it is also not tenable that there is difference", the Tibetan `lhan cig` taken temporally; `sārdham`, "together with, jointly with" (Monier-Williams), states the relation of the one thing to the other, as Kalupahana (1986) "jointly with another" has it.
[^122]: The DSBC prints `pratītyānyān`; read `pratītyānyan`, `anyat` before `nānyad`, the neuter singular of the verse's other `anyad` and of `tad anyat` in the second half.
[^123]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the second half "Because something depends upon something, a different thing is not appropriate", reading `tasmāt` as "therefore" and losing the correlation; `yat … yat … tasmāt tat` are correlatives, that (`tat`) which depends on something is not other than that (`tasmāt`) on which it depends, as Garfield (1995) reads it.
[^124]: `bhavet` has no predicate: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) supply "exist", "it would exist even without that other thing"; the page supplies the otherness just stated, "it would be so even apart from the other", the absurd consequence that the second half's `nāsti` then denies.
[^125]: `kathaṃ … nāma` is also the idiom "how indeed, how, pray" (Monier-Williams), Kalupahana (1986) "how could there be a self-nature that is made?", Garfield (1995) "How could it be appropriate for fabricated essence to come to be?"; `nāma` is read as "called", as the Tibetan `zhes bya ba`, "so-called", appears to read it, and the point, that a made `svabhāva` is a contradiction, is the same either way.
[^126]: Kalupahana (1986) makes `akṛtrimaḥ` an attribute, "an unmade self-nature is also non-contingent upon another"; `hi … ca` co-ordinate two predicates, "`svabhāva` is not artificial and is independent of another", which the question of the first half needs asserted, as Garfield (1995) reads it.
[^127]: In `svabhāvaḥ parabhāvasya parabhāvo hi kathyate` the first `parabhāvasya` is read by its parts, `parasya bhāvasya`, "of another existent", since the definition would otherwise define `parabhāva` by itself: so Kalupahana (1986) "self-nature of other-nature is called other-nature", and Garfield (1995) "the essence of difference in entities is what is called the entity of difference"; the Tibetan `gzhan gyi dngos po'i rang bzhin`, "the nature of another entity", reads it by its parts.
[^128]: Poussin's edition reads `svabhāve parabhāve vā sati`, whence Kalupahana (1986) "only when there is self-nature or other-nature"; the DSBC's `ca`, "and", is kept, and Garfield's (1995) "essences and entities" has the Tibetan's "and".
[^129]: The DSBC's `vā … vā` pairs the verse's two questions, "either … or"; Poussin's edition reads `ca … ca`, "and … and", and Kalupahana (1986) renders neither.
[^130]: The DSBC prints `nāśrīyet vicakṣaṇaḥ`, a syllable short and without the sandhi; read `nāśrīyeta`, as Poussin's edition prints it, the optative of ā-√śri, "rely on, resort to", Kalupahana (1986) "should not rely upon"; Garfield's (1995) "Does not say 'exists' or 'does not exist'" paraphrases the Tibetan, whose `brten` is "rely" too.
[^131]: `saṃskāras` in this chapter are the constructed things that transmigrate, whose `nirvāṇa` is examined (16.4) and that are bound or released (16.5), Kalupahana (1986) "dispositions", Garfield (1995) "compounded phenomena"; the glossary's "mental constructions" is kept.
[^132]: `upādāna` in this chapter is what is taken up in a birth, the `skandhas` left for the next (16.3), and the clinging that binds (16.6, 16.9), Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) "grasping"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept.
[^133]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `vibhavaḥ` "other-becoming" (his note explains it as "come to be non-existent") and `kiṃ saṃsariṣyati` "Wherein does he transmigrate?"; `vibhava` is Edgerton's bahuvrīhi "free from existence", not the "passing away" of chapter 21, as Garfield (1995) "nonexistent" reads it, and `kiṃ saṃsariṣyati`, "what will transmigrate?", answers 16.2's `kaḥ saṃsariṣyati`, "who will transmigrate?" (an adverbial `kim`, "why will it transmigrate?", is also possible).
[^134]: The DSBC prints `saṃsarriṣyati`; read `saṃsariṣyati`, as in 16.2 and as Poussin's edition prints it.
[^135]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "If it is assumed that bondage exists prior to the binding of that which is to be bound, that does not exist", folding `badhnīyād bandhanaṃ kāmam` into "if it is assumed" and taking `bandhyāt pūrvam` of the binding; Garfield (1995) "If prior to binding there is a bound one, there would be bondage", the Tibetan putting the bound one first. `bandhyāt` is the ablative of the gerundive, "before the one to be bound", `bandhanam` is the subject of `bhavet`, and `badhnīyād … kāmam` is the consequence granted for the argument, "bondage would bind, granted".
[^136]: The DSBC prints `bhavet yadi`, without the sandhi; read `bhaved yadi`, as Poussin's edition prints it.
[^137]: `upādānamahāgrahaḥ` is read as a genitive compound, "the great grasping of `upādāna`"; it may equally be a karmadhāraya, "the great grasp that is `upādāna`", the grasp at `nirvāṇa` being itself the `upādāna` that binds (16.6); Kalupahana (1986) "a gigantic grasping" and Garfield (1995) "a great grasping" leave `upādāna` unrendered.
[^138]: Garfield (1995) renders "When you can't bring about nirvāṇa, nor the purification of cyclic existence, what is cyclic existence, and what is the nirvāṇa you examine?", a paraphrase of the Tibetan; `samāropa`, "superimposition", and `apakarṣaṇa`, "removal", are the two acts that `na … na` denies, `yatra … tatra` is "where … there" and `vikalpyate` "is imagined", as Kalupahana (1986) "neither the attribution of freedom nor the elimination of the life-process … being discriminated" reads it.
[^139]: Kalupahana (1986), "this is the friendly way", attaches `maitram` to `dharmaḥ`; it is neuter and agrees with `cetaḥ`, the mind of goodwill, not with the masculine `dharmaḥ`, and Garfield (1995), "with a compassionate mind", keeps it with the mind.
[^140]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `aviratayaḥ` and `viratayaḥ` "associated with delight" and "with non-delight", as if from `rati`; `virati` is abstention (Monier-Williams `avirati` "incontinence"), and the two classes are the non-intimation of non-restraint and that of restraint.
[^141]: `dharma` here is a thing, one of the seven listed, and at 17.20 the teaching, the glossary's senses; "righteousness", the gloss of 17.1, is the sense there and at 17.11 only, the good as against `adharma`.
[^142]: The DSBC divides `tiṣṭhatyā pākakālāccet`; read `tiṣṭhaty ā pākakālāc cet`, `ā` with the ablative `pākakālāt`, "until the time of ripening".
[^143]: Kalupahana (1986), "Therefore, whatever thought-series there is, that proceeds from a thought", takes `tasmāt` as the adverb; it stands between `yaḥ` and the ablative `cetasaḥ` it agrees with, "from that mind", the mind of 17.1, answering 17.7's `bījāt`.
[^144]: Garfield (1995), "the method of realizing the Dharma", reads `dharmasya` as the teaching, which the Tibetan `chos` allows; a `dharma` whose means are the ten white paths of action and whose fruit is the five qualities of desire is the righteousness of 17.1, merit, Kalupahana (1986) "good".
[^145]: Kalupahana (1986) "which is appropriate" and Garfield (1995) "what is tenable here" read `yujyate`, "is fitting", the verb of 17.24; the DSBC and Poussin's edition both have `yojyate`, the causative passive "is applied", the conception that is to be put to the problem.
[^146]: Kalupahana (1986), "Like an imperishable promissory note, so is debt as well as action", makes `avipraṇāśa` an adjective of the note; `avipraṇāśaḥ` is masculine nominative against the neuter `pattram`, a noun, the non-perishing of 17.15–19, and `yathā … tathā … iva` pairs it with the document and `karman` with the debt. Garfield (1995) runs the two similes together, from the Tibetan.
[^147]: `dhātu` here is the realm, as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate it, the fourfold division being by the realms of desire, form and formlessness and the undefiled; the glossary's "component" is kept.
[^148]: Garfield (1995), "if action were destroyed through transformation", makes the transference destroy the action, with the Tibetan; `saṃkrameṇa vā` is a second instrument of `praheyaḥ syāt`, a second way the non-perishing might be abandoned, and the destruction of `karman` comes only among the faults that follow.
[^149]: The DSBC prints `pratisaṃghau`; read `pratisaṃdhau`, "at rebirth", as Poussin's edition has it and Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate ("at the moment of birth").
[^150]: Kalupahana (1986) "only one would arise" and Garfield (1995) "only one will arise" leave the subject open, as if one of the `karmans` arose; `saḥ` is the non-perishing of 17.14–16 and `ekaḥ` its predicate: one non-perishing arises at rebirth for all the `karmans` of the same realm, the genitives depending on it.
[^151]: `dṛṣṭe dharme` is the idiom for the present life (Edgerton `dṛṣṭadharma`, Pali `diṭṭhadhamma`), Kalupahana (1986) "in the present life", Garfield (1995) "in this visible world"; not the `dharma` of 17.1.
[^152]: Garfield (1995), "each comprising action and the unexpired separately, will remain while ripening", has the Tibetan; `karmaṇaḥ karmaṇaḥ` is the distributive genitive, one non-perishing for each `karman`, and `vipakve 'pi` the locative absolute with `api`, "even when ripened", as Kalupahana (1986) "stays so even when matured".
[^153]: Garfield (1995) makes the fruit the subject, "That fruit, if extinction or death occurs, ceases"; `saḥ` is masculine, the non-perishing, `phala` neuter, and the fruit sits inside the ablative `phalavyatikramāt`, as Kalupahana (1986) construes it.
[^154]: `anāsravaṃ sāsravaṃ` is "without `āsravas`" and "with `āsravas`", Kalupahana (1986) "without influxes" and "with influxes", Garfield (1995) "the stainless and the stained"; "defiled" keeps the glossary's "defilement" for `āsrava` (Pali `āsava`), the rendering that the `kleśa` (defilement) of 17.26 shares: two words under one English word.
[^155]: Kalupahana (1986) predicates, "Emptiness, however, is not annihilation; life-process is also not eternal"; `ca … na ca` coordinates, and the nominatives stand in apposition to `dharmo buddhena deśitaḥ`, the three things taught: emptiness and yet no annihilation, the cycle and yet no eternity, and the non-perishing, as Garfield (1995) also lists them.
[^156]: Garfield (1995) reverses reason and consequence, "Because action does not arise, it is seen to be without essence", with the Tibetan; `kasmāt … yatas tataḥ` asks why `karman` does not arise and answers with `niḥsvabhāvam`, as Kalupahana (1986) has it.
[^157]: Kalupahana (1986) reads two consequences, "An ignoble life as well as error would follow"; `prasajyate` is singular, `doṣaḥ` stands in apposition to `abrahmacaryavāsaḥ`, the one fault, and `ca` joins the second half to the first.
[^158]: Kalupahana (1986) "the distinction between the performance of merit and evil" and Garfield (1995) "between virtue and evil"; `puṇyapāpakṛtoḥ` is the genitive dual of the agent noun `-kṛt`, the two doers, whose distinction 17.28–30 takes up.
[^159]: Kalupahana (1986), "If action were to be determined, because it possesses self-nature", makes `svābhāvikam` the reason for `vyavasthitam`; `yasmāt … tasmāt` gives `karman` being fixed as the reason for the re-ripening, and `yadi svābhāvikam` closes the verse as the condition, as Garfield (1995) construes it.
[^160]: Kalupahana (1986) reads `cedaṃ` as `ced idam`, "If this action is associated with defilements"; `cet` before `idam` would give `ced idam`, so `cedaṃ` is `ca idam`, and the premise is stated, not supposed.
[^161]: Kalupahana (1986), "it would have the characteristics of the non-aggregates", analyses `askandhalakṣaṇa` as `askandha-lakṣaṇa`; the bahuvrīhi negates the possession: a self other than the `skandhas` would lack what they have, and so could not be apprehended, as Garfield (1995) also reads it.
[^162]: Garfield (1995) has no object in the second half, "One who does not grasp onto 'I' and 'mine,' he does not perceive"; `nirmamaṃ nirahaṃkāraṃ` are accusative, the object of `paśyati`: whoever sees someone as free of "mine" and "I" does not see, as Kalupahana (1986) construes it.
[^163]: `upādāna` here is the taking up that leads to birth, Kalupahana (1986) "grasping"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept. Garfield (1995) "the appropriator" has the Tibetan `nye bar len pa`, which serves the agent as well as the act, where `upādānam` is the neuter action noun, not the agent `upādātṛ`.
[^164]: Kalupahana (1986) "defilements of action" takes `karmakleśāḥ` as a tatpuruṣa, and "belong to one who discriminates" takes `vikalpataḥ` as a person; the compound is the dvandva of 17.27, `karma kleśāś ca`, both of them exhausted, and `vikalpataḥ` the ablative in `-taḥ` like 17.14's `dhātutaḥ`, as Garfield (1995) has it.
[^165]: Kalupahana (1986), "they have not spoken of something as the self or as the non-self", makes `na` govern `deśitam`; `iti` closes `nātmā na cānātmā kaścit` as the content taught, the third of three parallel predicates with `api`, as Garfield (1995) also reads it.
[^166]: The DSBC prints `nirvṛttam`; read `nivṛttam`, "has turned back", with Poussin's edition, the parallel `nivṛttaḥ` in the same line and the Tibetan `ldog pa`; Kalupahana (1986) "has ceased", Garfield (1995) "is nonexistent".
[^167]: Poussin's edition reads the locative absolute `nivṛtte cittagocare`, "when the range of mind has turned back", which Kalupahana (1986) follows, making the second clause the condition of the first; the DSBC's nominative `nivṛttaś cittagocaraḥ`, two coordinate statements, is kept, as Garfield (1995) also has them.
[^168]: `aparapratyaya` is the fixed adjective "not dependent on others" (Edgerton; Pali `aparappaccaya`), the `pratyaya` being reliance on another's word, Garfield (1995) "not dependent on another", Kalupahana (1986) "independently realized" from Candrakīrti's gloss; the glossary's "cause" for `pratyaya` does not reach inside the compound.
[^169]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `artha` as meaning, "without discriminations and a variety of meanings"; `artha` here is the thing, as in the benediction's `anekārtham anānārtham`, "not one thing, not different things", and 18.10 spells the pair out, "not that very thing" and "other than it"; Garfield (1995) "without distinctions".
[^170]: `anekārtham anānārtham` as in the benediction, summing up 18.10; Kalupahana (1986) "without a variety of meanings or one single meaning", Garfield (1995) "without identity, without distinction".
[^171]: `asaṃsargāt` is "from non-association", the solitary Buddhas' knowledge arising without keeping company with, and being taught by, others (Monier-Williams `saṃsarga` "association, intercourse"), Kalupahana (1986) "without association", Garfield (1995) "without a teacher", from the Tibetan; the glossary's "conjunction" for `saṃsarga`, chapter 14's word for the seer and the seen, is not the sense here.
[^172]: The DSBC prints `pratyutpanna’nāgataśca` in the second half; read `pratyutpanno ’nāgataś ca`, the nominative with its sandhi, as in 19.1–2 and Poussin's edition.
[^173]: `parivartakau` is "rotated in turn", the remaining two times each taken in turn as the point of reference, and with every other accusative of the verse it is the object of `lakṣayet`, "one should mark"; Kalupahana (1986) "the remaining two periods" and Garfield (1995) "the other two divisions" take the word in Edgerton's Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit sense "section".
[^174]: The relative `yo gṛhyeta` qualifies the enduring time of the clause before it, "an enduring time is not found which might be grasped", as the Tibetan has it, Garfield (1995) "nothing one could grasp as stationary time exists", and `agṛhītaś ca kālaḥ` opens the final question; Kalupahana (1986) "even if the unobserved time were to be observed" makes `agṛhīta` the subject of `gṛhyeta`, which nothing in the Sanskrit supports.
[^175]: The DSBC prints `datvā`; read `dattvā`, "having given", as in 20.6 `ādattvā` and Poussin's edition.
[^176]: `phalasya` is the recipient, "to the fruit", not "of the fruit", as the commentators and the Tibetan's dative `'bras bu la` take it: what is given, `yad dattam` in the second half, is the part of the primary cause handed to the fruit, and 20.6 `phalasyādattvā` is read the same way.
[^177]: `vṛtaḥ`, "enveloped" (`vṛ`, to cover, to surround), is a primary cause already joined with its fruit, the commentators' `saṃbaddha`, Garfield (1995) "joined with its effect", and the dilemma wants it so, since such a cause has nothing left to generate; Kalupahana (1986) "separated from the latter" takes `vṛta` as warded off.
[^178]: `katamat` is neuter and qualifies `phalam`, "which fruit", as Garfield (1995) has it; Kalupahana (1986) "what cause, even if it were not separated from the effect, will give rise to the effect" attaches it to the primary cause, and his "imperceptibly or perceptibly" paraphrases the absolutives `adṛṣṭvā … dṛṣṭvā`, whose agent is the primary cause. Poussin's edition reads `vā dṛṣṭvā vā` for the DSBC's `na dṛṣṭvāpi`, whence his "either … or"; the sense is the same.
[^179]: Kalupahana (1986) reverses subject and predicate in the second half, "For, the non-ceased and non-arisen will also be the non-empty", with a "for" the Sanskrit lacks; `tad` marks `aśūnyam` as the subject carried on from the first half, the non-empty fruit which will be unceased and unarisen, as Garfield (1995) reads it.
[^180]: The DSBC prints `ahetunāḥ`, with a stray visarga; read `ahetunā`, the instrumental "to what is not a primary cause", with Poussin's edition.
[^181]: `kiṃ` is neuter: it qualifies `phalam`, "what fruit", or is the question particle, "will a primary cause generate a fruit that …?", as Kalupahana (1986) takes it ("how is it that a cause will produce an effect …"), the same sense either way; Garfield (1995) "what could have caused it to arise" attaches it to the primary cause, which `hetuḥ`, masculine, does not allow.
[^182]: Kalupahana (1986) prints `anātmanā` and renders "not produced by itself or by another"; the DSBC, Poussin's edition and the Tibetan read `ātmānam ātmanā`, "itself by itself", as Garfield (1995) has it. The one negation and verb, `na … janayate`, span the two halves through the relative `yā … sā`, so "[generate]" is supplied in the first half and repeated by the second.
[^183]: `vinaiva janma maraṇam` is a nominal sentence, kept without a supplied verb: it states the consequence of the question before it, a death without a birth; Kalupahana (1986) carries the question over it ("how can there be dissolution without occurrence, death without birth …") and Garfield (1995) makes it a second question ("How could there be death without birth?").
[^184]: Poussin's edition reads `caivaṃ`, "and thus", in the second half, whence Kalupahana (1986) "similarly"; the DSBC's `caiva`, as in 21.5, is kept.
[^185]: `kṣaya` is taken of the existent that decays, "what decays", with Candrakīrti, Kalupahana (1986) "that which is waning" and Garfield (1995) "the disappeared"; the word's abstract sense, "the coming to be of decay", is also possible, but 21.8 makes an existent the locus of coming to be and passing away, and this verse asks of that existent whether it decays or not.
[^186]: Garfield (1995) renders `nityo ’nityo ’tha vā bhavet` "must be both permanent and impermanent"; `atha vā` is "or", the two horns of the dilemma, "either permanent or impermanent", as Kalupahana (1986) has it.
[^187]: The DSBC prints `bhāvabhyupapannasya`, a syllable short of the pāda's eight; read `bhāvam abhyupapannasya` as in 21.14 and Poussin's edition.
[^188]: `bhava` in 21.15–21 is the ongoing process of existence, the continuum of appearance and disappearance of fruit and primary cause that the verse defines, Kalupahana (1986) "becoming", Garfield (1995) "cyclic existence" (the Tibetan `srid pa`); the glossary's "existence" is kept.
[^189]: Kalupahana (1986) "with the repeated non-arising of that which ceases" attaches `punar` to the negation; `apunarutpatti` is "not arising again", as `apunarbhava` is not being born again, and Garfield (1995) "from the nonarising of the destroyed" reads it so.
[^190]: Garfield (1995) renders the second half "cyclic existence ceases completely, having been pacified", without `ucchedaḥ`; the Sanskrit asserts annihilation at the time of `nirvāṇa` as the consequence pressed on the view of 21.15, Kalupahana (1986) "there will be annihilation".
[^191]: `sārdham`, "together", opening the second half, is construed with `yujyate` of the first, as the Tibetan has it (`lhan cig` in the protasis): the one ceasing and the one being born are not fitting together, at one time; Kalupahana (1986) "would also be dying at the same time" and Garfield (1995) "whoever is dying with the aggregates" take it into the consequence, as the `ca` after it invites, and the point, death and birth at once in the same `skandhas`, is the same either way.
[^192]: The DSBC prints `skandhanupādāya`; read `skandhān upādāya`, "taking up the `skandhas`": `buddhaḥ skandhānupādāya` is eight syllables where `skandhānanupādāya` would be nine, and Poussin's edition, Candrakīrti and the Tibetan all read `upādāya`, as Kalupahana (1986) "dependent upon the aggregates" and Garfield (1995) "depended on the aggregates" translate it. The Buddha's dependence on the `skandhas` is the premise that 22.3 takes up (`pratītya parabhāvaṃ yaḥ`).
[^193]: Garfield (1995) puts the negation on the verb, "Its selfhood is not appropriate", with the Tibetan; in the Sanskrit it stands inside the predicate, `sa nātmety upapadyate` (Poussin's edition `so 'nātmety`, the compound `anātmā`), "that he is not a self is possible", as Kalupahana (1986) "would appropriately be without self".
[^194]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the gerund a cause, "if there were to be a tathāgata because of non-grasping on to the aggregates"; `anupādāya` is the circumstance of the hypothesis, a `tathāgata` who has not taken up the `skandhas`, as 22.6's parallel `anupādāya nāsti` shows and as Garfield (1995) "without depending on the aggregates" has it.
[^195]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `yaś ca nāsty anupādāya` "he who does not exist dependently", dropping the privative, and `upādāsyate` as a passive, "be grasped"; the future middle is "he will take up", its subject `sa`, the `tathāgata`, and its object the `skandhas` of the first line, as Garfield (1995) "come to be so [dependent]" also has it, though his first half, "no Tathāgata dependent upon the aggregates", loses the `an-` in turn.
[^196]: `upādāna` in this chapter is what is taken up in a birth, the `skandhas`, Kalupahana (1986) "grasping", Garfield (1995) "appropriation" and "grasping"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept, as is "taker of fuel" for `upādātṛ` in 22.10.
[^197]: Kalupahana (1986) cuts the first line into two items, "no sphere of non-grasping, nor … something as grasping", and both he and Garfield (1995) make the second line a question, "How can there be a tathāgata?"; the Sanskrit is two statements, `anupādattam upādānaṃ na bhavati kiṃcana`, "there is no `upādāna` whatsoever that is not taken up", and `na cāsti nirupādānaḥ kathaṃcana tathāgataḥ`, "in no way is there a `tathāgata` without `upādāna`", `kathaṃcana` with `na` being "in any way", not "how". Garfield's "There is no appropriator" answers to no word of the verse.
[^198]: Kalupahana (1986) reads the dvandva `tattvānyatvena` as "in the form of a different identity"; it is "by way of identity or otherness", the first two of the five ways of 22.1, as Garfield (1995) "neither identical nor different". `tattva` here is identity, being that very thing, not the glossary's `tattva` (reality).
[^199]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `grāho gṛhītaḥ` as two things, "on the basis of grasping or the grasped", and joins "exists or does not exist" to the firm grasp; Garfield (1995) has the grasp "exists" but nothing for `nāstīti`. In the Sanskrit `astīti tathāgataḥ` is the content of the grasp and `nāstīti` the content of `vikalpayan`: having grasped that the `tathāgata` exists, one would imagine "he does not exist" even of the extinguished one. Poussin's edition has the same words in another order, `yena grāho gṛhītas tu ghano 'stīti tathāgataḥ`, with `ghana` an adjective of the grasp, which leaves the sense unchanged.
[^200]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the distortions the subject of the second line, "Perversions regarding the pleasant and the unpleasant arise depending upon these"; `śubhāśubhaviparyāsān` is accusative, the object of `pratītya`, and the subject of `saṃbhavanti` is passion, hatred and delusion, as Garfield (1995) has it. The Tibetan reads the compound as a dvandva of three, Garfield "the pleasant, the unpleasant, and errors" (so again in 23.2 and 23.6); the sense, passion from the distortion "pleasant" and hatred from "unpleasant" (23.12), and Candrakīrti want the tatpuruṣa "the distortions of the pleasant and the unpleasant".
[^201]: Kalupahana (1986) again makes the distortions the subject, "Whatever perversions of the pleasant and the unpleasant that occur dependently are not evident"; the accusative `viparyāsān` is the object of `pratītya`, and `ye … te` are the `kleśas` that depend on them, as Garfield (1995) "whatever depends on the pleasant and the unpleasant".
[^202]: The DSBC prints `svabhāvāanna`, with a doubled vowel; read `svabhāvān na`, as Poussin's edition.
[^203]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) make the second line a statement, "in the absence of anyone, these defilements seem to exist without belonging to anyone", "Without that possessor, The defilements are nobody's"; `āho` is an interjection of asking and of doubt (Monier-Williams), and opens the alternative question the page keeps.
[^204]: Garfield (1995) again reads the compound as the Tibetan's dvandva, "The pleasant, the unpleasant, and the errors"; Kalupahana (1986) and the page read "the distortions of the pleasant and the unpleasant", as at 23.1.
[^205]: `sparśa` in the list of the six objects (`rūpa`, `śabda`, `rasa`, `sparśa`, `gandha`, `dharma`) is the tangible, the object of the sense of touch (Monier-Williams; the `spraṣṭavya`), as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) "touch"; the glossary's "contact" is the sense of the link `sparśa` and is not read here or in 23.8. `dharmas` in the same list are the objects of the mind, the glossary's "phenomena", Kalupahana and Garfield "concepts".
[^206]: The DSBC's `doṣasya` is the Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit form of `dveṣasya`, "of hatred" (Edgerton), not "of fault"; Poussin's edition prints `dveṣasya`.
[^207]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `kevalāḥ` as "all these" (Monier-Williams "entire, whole"); "mere" is its other sense, which the illusory persons and reflections of 23.9 want, as Garfield (1995) "only".
[^208]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "in people who are fabrications of illusion", with `puruṣa` as the head of the compound and `kalpa` "fabrication"; `kalpa` at the end of a compound is "resembling, like", and `teṣu` are the six objects of 23.8, "in those which resemble illusory persons", as Garfield (1995) "those that are like an illusory person".
[^209]: The DSBC prints `śubha nāstyaśubhaṃ`, the anusvāra lost; read `śubhaṃ nāsty aśubhaṃ`, as Poussin's edition and the full form `anapekṣyāśubhaṃ nāsti śubhaṃ` of 23.11.
[^210]: Where `prajñapayemahi` attaches is open: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) give it the first clause, "We make known that the unpleasant does not exist without being contingent upon the pleasant", the page `yat pratītya śubham`, "we would designate the pleasant depending on it", and so in 23.11; both are grammatical, and the point, the mutual dependence of the pleasant and the unpleasant, is the same either way. The Tibetan has no clause for the pleasant's dependence on the unpleasant and derives "the pleasant is not tenable" from the unpleasant's dependence alone, which the Sanskrit does not say.
[^211]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the first line a conditional with its own consequence, "if there were to be grasping on to the view … then there is perversion", and the last words two nouns, "how can there be grasping or perversion?"; `grāho viparyayaḥ` is subject and predicate, "the grasp is a distortion", as 23.14's `grāhaḥ … kiṃ na viparyayaḥ` (which Kalupahana so renders) and as Garfield (1995) "How could that grasping be an error?".
[^212]: Garfield (1995) makes the content of the grasp "In emptiness there is nothing impermanent", 23.13's own `nānityaṃ vidyate śūnye`, with the Tibetan; the Sanskrit `anityam ity api grāhaḥ śūnye` is the grasp "impermanent" with regard to what is empty, the counterpart of "permanent" with regard to the impermanent, as Kalupahana (1986) "What is empty is impermanent".
[^213]: The DSBC encloses this verse in square brackets; Poussin's edition prints it, Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) translate it, and it is translated like the rest.
[^214]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `bhūtāḥ` as the participle, "defilements … have come to be on the basis of self-nature"; with `abhūtāḥ` and `asadbhāvam` in 23.25 the sense is Edgerton's "real, true", as Garfield (1995) "existed through his essence".
[^215]: `sākṣikarma`, "personal realisation", is the fourth of the tasks on the four `āryasatya`, which Candrakīrti lists as the full understanding of `duḥkha`, the giving up of its origin, the development of the path and the personal realisation of cessation (`duḥkhasatyaparijñānaṃ … samudayasya prahāṇaṃ … pratipado bhāvanā … nirodhasya sākṣikarma`); the glossary's "realisation" for `āryasatya` itself, Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) "noble truth", is kept, so that "realisation" stands in this verse in two senses.
[^216]: Poussin's edition reads `catvāry āryaphalāni`, "the four noble fruits", as Kalupahana (1986) translates; the DSBC's `catvāry api phalāni`, "the four fruits too", is kept, being the wording of 24.27 in the DSBC and in Poussin's edition alike, and Garfield (1995) too has "the four fruits" without "noble".
[^217]: Garfield (1995) makes the eight persons a second consequence of the Saṃgha's non-existence, "nor will the eight kinds of person", in the Tibetan's order; the Sanskrit's `na cet santi te 'ṣṭau puruṣapudgalāḥ` is the condition, "if those eight … do not exist", as Kalupahana (1986) also has it.
[^218]: The DSBC prints `pratibāghase`; read `pratibādhase`, "you reject", the verb of 24.6, 24.17, 24.23 and 24.36, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^219]: `śūnyatāṃ` goes with `bruvāṇaḥ` of 24.5, "speaking of `śūnyatā`", as Candrakīrti states outright (`śūnyatāṃ bruvāṇa ity anena saṃbandhaḥ`) and as Kalupahana (1986), "speaking in this manner about emptiness", and Garfield (1995), whose Tibetan carries the word into 24.5, take it: the opponent's charge is that asserting emptiness destroys the fruit, merit and the conventions, which 24.36 turns back, not that emptiness is rejected; the verb `pratibādhase`, last in the Sanskrit, comes forward in English.
[^220]: `dharma` here and in 24.33–24.35, paired with `adharma`, is merit and demerit, Candrakīrti's `dharmādharmau` with their desired and undesired fruits (`saha taddhetukena iṣṭāniṣṭaphalena`), not the glossary's teaching or phenomenon; Kalupahana (1986) "good and bad", Garfield (1995) "right or wrong actions".
[^221]: Kalupahana (1986) attaches `śūnyatāṃ śūnyatārthaṃ ca` to `vihanyase`, "you are tormented by emptiness and the meaning of emptiness"; the accusatives are objects of `vetsi` with `prayojanam` (a passive "tormented by" would want instrumentals), as Garfield (1995) also takes them, and Candrakīrti on 24.13 names the three things not understood, `śūnyatāṃ śūnyatārthaṃ śūnyatāprayojanaṃ ca`.
[^222]: Poussin's edition reads `gambhīraṃ`, with `tattvam`, "the profound `tattva`", as Kalupahana (1986) translates and as Garfield's (1995) "the Buddha's profound truth" suggests of the Tibetan; the DSBC's `gambhīre`, qualifying `buddhaśāsane`, "the profound instruction", is kept, the sense barely changing.
[^223]: The DSBC prints `mandameghasam`; read `mandamedhasam`, "one of dull intellect" (`manda-medhas`), as Poussin's edition has it.
[^224]: `vidyā` in the simile is the spell or magical science (`duṣprasādhitā`, "wrongly mastered", is said of the `sādhana` of a spell), as Garfield (1995) "a spell incorrectly cast" has it with the Tibetan `rig sngags`, and as Candrakīrti's illustration of the verse, the jewel, the spell and the herb (`maṇi`, `mantra`, `auṣadha`) that harm when wrongly used, bears out; Kalupahana (1986) "knowledge that is wrongly cultivated".
[^225]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `adhilaya` "obsession" and makes the `sa` of the second half that obsession, "that [obsession] is not appropriate in the context of the empty"; Edgerton's dictionary cites this verse under `adhilaya` "objection, refutation", Candrakīrti glosses it `adhikṣepaṃ nirākaraṇaṃ pratikṣepam` and continues of the `doṣaprasaṅga`, which is the `sa` ("it is not possible in the empty"), and Garfield (1995) too has "refutations".
[^226]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) make the second half a simile, "like one who has mounted his horse"; the verse has no `yathā` or `iva` (`aśvam evāsi vismṛtaḥ`, "you have forgotten"), and the rider is Candrakīrti's explanation of it (`yathā hi kaścid … evam eva bhavān`).
[^227]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `ahetupratyayān` "non-causal conditions"; it is the `bahuvrīhi`, "without primary cause and cause", as Garfield (1995) also has it and as Candrakīrti glosses, `avidyamānahetupratyayān padārthān … nirhetukān`.
[^228]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `karaṇam` "performance of action"; it is the instrument, the `kāraka` beside the agent and the action, which Candrakīrti illustrates with the potter's wheel (`cakrādikasya karaṇasya`) beside the potter and the act of making.
[^229]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `sā prajñaptir upādāya` "that is dependent upon convention", as if `prajñapti` were the object of `upādāya`; `prajñaptiḥ` is nominative, the predicate, "that is a `prajñapti` in dependence" (`upādāya prajñaptiḥ`), as Garfield (1995) "a dependent designation" has it and as Candrakīrti settles it: `saiva śūnyatā upādāya prajñaptir iti vyavasthāpyate`.
[^230]: The DSBC prints `kato`; read `kuto`, "how" (or "whence"), as Poussin's edition has it.
[^231]: `svabhāvaparyavasthānāt` is the opponent's holding to `svabhāva`, not its immutability: `paryavasthāna` is "obstinate resistance" and, in Buddhist Sanskrit, "possession, obsession" (Edgerton), and Candrakīrti glosses the compound of the opponent, `svabhāvaṃ gṛhītvā pratyavatiṣṭhamānaḥ`, grasping `svabhāva` and standing firm on it; Kalupahana (1986) "by adhering to a notion of self-nature", Garfield (1995) "if an essence is posited".
[^232]: Kalupahana (1986) supplies "[i.e., the path]" and Garfield (1995) has "it cannot have an essence", of the path; `svābhāvyaṃ te na vidyate` is general, and the path is Candrakīrti's application of it (`svabhāvatā āryamārgasya na syāt`), which the general statement covers.
[^233]: `duḥkhanirodhatvāt`, an ablative in `-tvāt`, gives the ground or motive, not the means: the path is what leads to the cessation, its end, as Candrakīrti has it, `duḥkhasya nirodhaprāptyarthaṃ … bhāvanā mārgasyeṣyate`; Garfield (1995) "to obtain the cessation of suffering" reads it so, Kalupahana (1986) "through the cessation of suffering" as the means. The object of `prāpayiṣyati` is unexpressed; "to attainment" supplies it.
[^234]: `tasya` is of `duḥkha`, as Candrakīrti has it (`aparijñātasvabhāvasya duḥkhasya … parijñānam`); Garfield (1995) attaches it to the `svabhāva`, "through its essence".
[^235]: `punaḥ` here and in 24.28 is "later": what is not understood by `svabhāva` cannot be understood afterwards, since `svabhāva` does not change (the second half), and Candrakīrti paraphrases `tat paścāt parijñāyata iti kalpyate, tad ayuktam`; Kalupahana (1986) "subsequently", Garfield (1995) omits the word.
[^236]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `prahāṇasākṣātkaraṇe` as a locative, "in relation to the activities of relinquishing, realizing", with "this [explanation]" as the subject; it is the dual nominative, subject with `bhāvanā` and `phalāni` of the plural `yujyante`, as Garfield (1995) also has it and as Candrakīrti reads: `te ete dve prahāṇasākṣātkaraṇe yā ca mārgasya bhāvanā, eṣāpi … na yujyante`.
[^237]: The Sanskrit Kalupahana (1986) prints reads `svabhāvenādhigataṃ`, lacking the `an-` of every edition, and he translates "a fruit that has already been realized through self-nature"; the DSBC, Poussin's edition and the Tibetan (Garfield (1995) "already unrealized") read `anadhigataṃ`, "unattained", with Candrakīrti's `pūrvam anadhigatasvabhāvānāṃ paścād apy adhigamo nopapadyate`, parallel to 24.26; `punaḥ` is "later" as there.
[^238]: Kalupahana (1986) takes the genitive `aśūnyasya` as the agent, "what could the non-empty do?", and renders the passive `kriyate` as active, "self-nature does not perform"; the genitive is the object and the verb is of making, as Candrakīrti reads them, `kiṃ hi aśūnyasya kartavyam? na hi svabhāvasyāśūnyasya kāraṇam upapadyate vidyamānatvāt`, nothing can be made of what is not empty, because it already exists; Garfield (1995) "what could one do", "cannot be produced".
[^239]: `nimitta` here and in 24.35 is the occasion or ground of the fruit, "occasioned by" (so Kalupahana (1986)), not the glossary's "sign".
[^240]: Garfield (1995) makes the second half a conditional, "if the fruit arose from right or wrong actions, according to you, it wouldn't exist"; `dharmādharmanimittaṃ ca phalaṃ tava na vidyate` has no `yadi`, and Kalupahana (1986) and Candrakīrti (`tadā dharmādharmajaṃ phalaṃ tava na saṃbhavati`) read the plain statement.
[^241]: The DSBC prints `lokikān` where 24.6 and Poussin's edition have `laukikān`, "worldly"; Edgerton records `lokika` as a Buddhist Sanskrit spelling, so it may be genuine, and it is read as `laukikān`.
[^242]: Poussin's edition reads `yat pratītyasamutpādaśūnyatāṃ pratibādhase`, "in that you reject the `śūnyatā` of `pratītyasamutpāda`", on which Candrakīrti comments (`yacchabdaḥ kriyāviśeṣaṇam`, the word `yat` qualifies the verb) and which Kalupahana (1986) renders "when you contradict"; the DSBC's `yaḥ`, "you who reject", is kept, the sense being the same.
[^243]: The subject of `yady aśūnyam` is unexpressed: Kalupahana (1986) supplies "the non-empty [is evident]", Garfield (1995) "(the world)" from 24.38; Candrakīrti supplies all this, `yadi hi aśūnyaṃ sasvabhāvaṃ sarvam etat syāt`, the `idaṃ sarvam` of 24.1 and 24.20, which "it" carries.
[^244]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `iṣyate` "does one expect freedom" and Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "does one wish nirvāṇa to arise", the root's first sense; here and in 25.2 the verb has its sense in debate, "is held, admitted", as in Candrakīrti's introduction to the verse, `kleśagaṇasya prahāṇāt sopadhiśeṣaṃ nirvāṇam iṣyate`: the opponent asks of what the Mādhyamika can hold `nirvāṇa` to be the abandoning or the cessation.
[^245]: `anupādāya` has no object in the Sanskrit; what is taken up is, in Candrakīrti's gloss, the assemblage of a thing's own causes (`tad upādāya bhavet, svakāraṇasāmagrīm āśritya bhavet`), so that Kalupahana (1986) renders the gerund "independent" and Garfield (1995) "nondependent"; "taking up", the verb of the glossary's `upādāna` "fuel", is kept without an object, as in chapter 22.
[^246]: Garfield (1995) carries the `nirvāṇam` that opens the third pāda into the second half, "Where nirvāṇa is not existent, it cannot be a nonexistent"; the verse has the shape of 25.6 and 25.8, whose third pāda opens with the `nirvāṇam` of the question in the same way, and Candrakīrti restates the first half as `kim abhāvo bhaviṣyati nirvāṇam? abhāvo 'pi na bhaviṣyati`, the second half being the general rule, as Kalupahana (1986) reads it.
[^247]: Kalupahana (1986) makes two sentences of the verse, "Whatever is of the nature of coming and going that occurs contingently or dependently. Freedom is, therefore, indicated as being non-contingent and independent", with "therefore" supplied and `saḥ` dropped; `saḥ` is the subject of `upadiśyate`, that same moving to and fro, when not depending and not taking up, being taught to be `nirvāṇa`, as Garfield (1995) reads and as Candrakīrti says: `asya janmamaraṇaparaṃparāprabandhasya apratītya vā anupādāya vā apravṛttis tan nirvāṇam`.
[^248]: Garfield (1995) makes `nirvāṇa` the subject and `ubhayam` its object, "since it would depend on both of these", the Tibetan's turn; `ubhayaṃ … tat` is the pair itself, each of the two taking up (Candrakīrti: `bhāvam upādāya abhāvaḥ, abhāvaṃ copādāya bhāvaḥ, iti kṛtvā ubhayam etad … upādāyaiva bhavati, na anupādāya`), which Kalupahana (1986) renders "existence and non-existence are, indeed, dependent upon one another".
[^249]: Poussin's edition reads the second half `na tayor ekatrāstitvam ālokatamasor yathā`, "there is no existence of those two in one place, as of light and darkness", which Kalupahana (1986) translates, and Candrakīrti's text has `tayor ekatra nāstitvam`; the DSBC's `tayor abhāvo hy ekatra prakāśatamasor iva` is kept, the same point in other words.
[^250]: Garfield (1995) "by whom is it expounded" asks for a knower, Kalupahana (1986) "by means of what is it made known as neither existence nor non-existence" for an instrument, with `tad` as `nirvāṇa` and `iti` marking what is made known; Candrakīrti asks both, whether such a `nirvāṇa` has anyone to apprehend it (`kaścid evaṃvidhaḥ pratipattāsti`) and whether it is grasped by `vijñāna` or by `jñāna` (`kena … ajyate gṛhyate prakāśate vā`), and "by what", with "that" pointing at the quoted words, leaves both open.
[^251]: The DSBC prints `naivabhāvo naiva bhāva iti`; read `naivābhāvo naiva bhāva iti`, "neither a non-existent nor an existent", as the first half of the verse and 25.15 have it and as Poussin's edition and Candrakīrti's text read.
[^252]: Poussin's edition reads `nohyate`, "is not conceived" (`√ūh`), in this verse and in 25.18, as does Candrakīrti's text, whose commentary glosses it `na gṛhyate` and sums up 25.18 with `yathā nājyaṃ na cohyaṃ`, naming both verbs; Kalupahana (1986) "it is not assumed" translates it. The DSBC's `nājyate`, "is not made clear" (`√añj`, the verb of 25.16 in both editions), is kept; Garfield's (1995) "is neither said to be" is the Tibetan, which stands nearer the DSBC's verb.
[^253]: Kalupahana (1986) takes `antaram` as "between" and `kiṃcit susūkṣmam` as the subject, "between them not even a subtle something is evident", and Garfield (1995) gives both, "not even the slightest difference between them, or even the subtlest thing"; `antara` "difference" takes up 25.19's `viśeṣaṇa`, Candrakīrti's comment turns on `saṃsārasya nirvāṇenāviśiṣṭatvāt`, `saṃsāra` being undistinguished from `nirvāṇa`, and "between" would leave the genitive `tayoḥ` with nothing to govern it.
[^254]: Garfield (1995), in the Tibetan's order, folds the first two groups of views into one, "views that after cessation there is a limit, etc."; Kalupahana (1986) attaches "after death" to all of them. Candrakīrti keeps three groups on three bases: the four views on the Tathāgata after cessation `nirvāṇaparāmarśena pravṛttāḥ`, the four on the finite world `aparāntaṃ samāśritya`, the four on the eternal world `pūrvāntaṃ samāśritāḥ`, as in 27.1–2.
[^255]: Kalupahana (1986) reads `kim` as "why" and supplies the verb, "why [speculate on] the finite, the infinite …", here and in 25.23; `anantam`, `antavat` and 25.23's `śāśvatam` are neuter predicates of `kim`, what could be infinite when all `dharmas` are empty, as Garfield (1995) reads and as Candrakīrti puts it: `caturdaśāpy etāni avyākṛtavastūni asati bhāvasvarūpe naiva yujyante`.
[^256]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the first half the object of `deśitaḥ`, "The Buddha did not teach the appeasement of all objects, the appeasement of obsession, and the auspicious as some thing to some one at some place", with `śivaḥ` a third item; Candrakīrti reads the first half as `nirvāṇa` described (`sarveṣāṃ prapañcānāṃ nimittānāṃ ya upaśamo 'pravṛttis tan nirvāṇam`), the quieting itself auspicious because quiet by nature (`sa eva copaśamaḥ prakṛtyaivopaśāntatvāc chivaḥ`), and the second half as what follows when the Buddhas abide in it, no `dharma`, defiled or purified, taught to anyone anywhere, as Garfield (1995) also reads; the first half repeats the benediction's `prapañcopaśamaṃ śivam`.
[^257]: `saṃskāra` here is the second link, the actions that one covered by `avidyā` performs towards a new birth, in Candrakīrti's words `trividhān karmalakṣaṇān saṃskārān`, wholesome, unwholesome and imperturbable, or of body, speech and mind (`kuśalā akuśalā ānejyāś ca … kāyikā vācikā mānasāś ceti`); Kalupahana (1986) "dispositions", Garfield (1995) "the three kinds of actions which as dispositions impel one"; the glossary's "mental constructions" is kept.
[^258]: `āgamya`, the gerund of `ā-gam`, is in Buddhist Sanskrit a postposition, "owing to, because of, on account of" (Edgerton), as Pāḷi `āgamma`; Candrakīrti's paraphrase of 26.5 uses the same idiom, `sparśam āgamya`, and restates it as `sparśahetukā vedanā`, feeling caused by contact. Kalupahana (1986) "depending upon" and Garfield (1995) "depending on" render it with the word of `pratītya` in 26.4; "owing to" keeps the two apart.
[^259]: Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, "craving arises because of feeling", which the first words of the verse have said already; `vedanārtham` is the purpose, feeling being what one craves for, as Kalupahana (1986) "feeling-directed" and Candrakīrti: `vedanānimittam eva abhilāṣaṃ karoti … tasyāḥ punaḥ punaḥ saṃyogārthaṃ paritṛṣyate`, one craves union again and again with a pleasant feeling.
[^260]: `upādāna` here is the ninth link, the fourfold taking up, of sense pleasures, of views, of rules and observances and of a doctrine of self (Candrakīrti: `kāmadṛṣṭiśīlavratātmavādopādānākhyaṃ caturvidhaṃ karmākṣepakāraṇam`, "the fourfold cause that projects action"), Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) "grasping"; the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept, with "the taker of fuel" for 26.7's `upādātṛ`.
[^261]: Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan's recasting, makes the taker come to be, "When there is grasping, the grasper comes into existence … being freed, he would not come into existence"; `bhavaḥ`, the tenth link, is the subject of `pravartate` and `upādātuḥ` its genitive, as Kalupahana (1986) reads, "becoming on the part of the grasper proceeds", and Candrakīrti glosses: `tasya upādātuḥ upādānapratyayo bhavaḥ upajāyate … tadānīṃ tasya na bhaved bhavaḥ`.
[^262]: Kalupahana (1986) reads the compound as "suffering relating to decay and death, etc."; the formula of the links has `duḥkha` as an item of its own between `parideva` and `daurmanasya`, and Candrakīrti glosses the items one by one with `duḥkha` separate, `pañcendriyāsātanipāto duḥkham, manoniṣṭanipāto daurmanasyam`, their explanation being as in the sūtra (`eṣāṃ ca yathāsūtram eva vyākhyānaṃ veditavyam`).
[^263]: Poussin's edition and Candrakīrti's text read `saṃsāramūlān`, an adjective of the `saṃskāras`, as Kalupahana (1986) translates, "dispositions that constitute the source of the life process"; the DSBC's neuter `saṃsāramūlaṃ` is kept, in apposition. Garfield (1995), with the Tibetan, has "The root of cyclic existence is action. Therefore, the wise one does not act", where the Sanskrit has `avidvān saṃskaroti`, "the one who does not know constructs".
[^264]: Poussin's edition and Candrakīrti's text read `jñānenāsyaiva bhāvanāt`, "from the development, through knowledge, of just this", the dependent origination just set out (Candrakīrti: `asyaiva pratītyasamutpādasya yathāvad aviparītabhāvanātaḥ avidyā prahīyate`), which Kalupahana (1986) renders with a bracket of his own, "the practice of that [non-occurrence of dispositions] through wisdom"; Garfield's (1995) "through meditation and wisdom" is the Tibetan. On the DSBC's genitive `jñānasya` the `asya` goes with the knowledge, "of this very knowledge", and that is kept.
[^265]: Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) make the 'eternal world' views a second set beside 'I was', 'I was not' (Garfield "And the view that the world is permanent, etc.", with the Tibetan; Kalupahana "views asserting an eternal world, etc. based upon [the perception]: 'Did I exist or not exist in the past?'", his supplement to Poussin's first line, which Poussin reconstructed from the Tibetan as a question, `dṛṣṭayo ’bhūvaṃ nābhūvaṃ kiṃ nv atīte ’dhvanī ti ca`). The DSBC's line, a statement, has no conjunction, and `yās tāḥ śāśvatalokādyāḥ` identifies the two: 'I was', 'I was not' are themselves the 'eternal world' views, as 27.3–8 and 27.15–18 treat them.
[^266]: `pūrvānta`, "the past" (and `aparānta` in 27.2, "the future"), is Edgerton's whole-word entry, Kalupahana (1986) "the prior end [of existence]", Garfield (1995) "a prior limit"; the glossary's "extreme" for `anta` is not applied inside the compound, whose "end" is the one that `antavān lokaḥ`, "the world is finite", takes up in 27.21–28 and that `antādyāḥ` in 27.2, "'finite' and so on", abbreviates.
[^267]: The `bhaviṣyāmi` that opens the second line completes the question `kim anyo ’nāgate ’dhvani`, and `iti ca` closes it, so the views resting on the future are two, as 27.14 pairs them and as Kalupahana (1986) and Garfield (1995) read.
[^268]: `upādāna` in this chapter is what is taken up in a birth, the aggregates, Kalupahana (1986) "grasping", Garfield (1995) "appropriation" ("appropriator" here, with the Tibetan); the glossary's "fuel", from chapter 10's simile, is kept, with "taker of fuel" for `upādātṛ` in 27.6 and 27.26.
[^269]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `upādānaṃ viśiṣyate` "then grasping is identified", as if "is specified"; the passive of `vi-śiṣ` is "is distinguished, differs", the point being that the `upādāna` of one birth differs from that of another, so that a self freed from `upādāna` is then asked for, as Garfield (1995) has it ("the appropriator is different").
[^270]: Garfield (1995) has "How can one accept that future appropriating is the appropriator?", the Tibetan's rendering of the future tense as an adjective; `bhaviṣyati` is the verb, "how will the `upādāna` be the taker", as Kalupahana (1986) reads it ("how can grasping be the grasper?").
[^271]: Kalupahana (1986) reads the second half as two statements, "A person who is without grasping can be observed. However, if he were to be different [from grasping], he could not be observed", an empiricist reading he defends against Candrakīrti in his note; `yadi` governs the optative `gṛhyeta`, "if it were other, it would be apprehended without `upādāna`", and the indicative `na ca gṛhyate` states the fact, as Garfield (1995) has it in substance.
[^272]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "A self does not exist. Yet, it is not the case that a person who does not grasp does not exist", moving `anupādānaḥ` to the second clause to make a positive non-grasper, on the ground of Kumārajīva's Chinese, as his note says; `anupādānaḥ` stands beside `ātmā nāsti` as its predicate, "there is no self without `upādāna`", and `nāpi nāsti`, "nor does it not exist", has that self as its subject, as Garfield (1995) reads it ("There is no self without appropriation. But it is not true that it does not exist").
[^273]: Garfield (1995) has nothing for `tathaiva ca sa saṃtiṣṭhet`, "and that one would remain just so", beyond "If this were so"; Kalupahana (1986) keeps the clause ("he would remain the same").
[^274]: The DSBC prints `jāyet cāmṛtaḥ`, a syllable short; read `jāyeta`, the optative, as Poussin's edition has it (`jāyeta vāmṛtaḥ`, "or" for the DSBC's "and").
[^275]: Kalupahana (1986) renders `amṛtaḥ` as a substantive, "an immortal would emerge"; with `sa saṃtiṣṭhet`, "that one would remain just so", the absurdity is a birth without a death, "it would be born not having died", as Garfield (1995) has it ("Without death, one would be born").
[^276]: Poussin's edition reconstructs the verse from the Tibetan, as Kalupahana (1986) notes, reading `ucchedaḥ karmaṇāṃ nāśas tathā ’nyena kṛtakarmaṇām anyena paribhogaḥ syād evam ādi prasajyate`, with the same sense; Kalupahana's "the fruit of action performed by one will be experienced by another" and Garfield's (1995) "Different agents' actions would be experienced by each other" render that text, and the DSBC's `kṛtam anyena karma ca pratisaṃvedayed anyaḥ`, "`karman` done by one another would experience", is kept.
[^277]: Kalupahana (1986) renders "Yet, in that context, the error of assuming an emergent without prior existence does not follow", attaching the negation to `prasajyate`, a reading that serves his point about the Sautrāntika `abhūtvā bhāva`; `nāpi` negates `abhūtvā samudbhūtaḥ`, "nor has it arisen without having been", and `hi` gives the reason, "for here a fault follows", the fault the second half then states, made or without a primary cause, as Garfield (1995) reads it.
[^278]: The DSBC prints `sa devaṃ sa manuṣyaḥ`; read `sa devaḥ`, the nominative parallel to `sa manuṣyaḥ`, as Poussin's edition has it.
[^279]: The DSBC prints `śāsvatam`; read `śāśvatam`, as the second line spells it.
[^280]: Kalupahana (1986) makes the second half a condition and its result, "If the divine being were to be non-arisen, then he would not be born and that would constitute the eternal"; it is a statement and its reason, `hi`: "and the god would be unarisen, for the eternal is not born", as Garfield (1995) has it ("The god would be unborn. For any permanent thing is unborn").
[^281]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the first half "If it is thought that a human is the same as a divine being, then there would be the non-eternal", a slip: Poussin's text, as he prints it, has `devād anyo manuṣyaś ced`, "if the human is other than the god", in both lines, as the DSBC does.
[^282]: The DSBC prints `manuṣyaśced saṃtatir`, `ced` before `s-` where sandhi wants `cet`; Poussin's edition, as Kalupahana (1986) prints it, has the same.
[^283]: The DSBC prints `aśāsvataṃ`; read `aśāśvataṃ`, as Poussin's edition and 27.18 have it.
[^284]: Kalupahana (1986) renders the second half "then it is not possible to either assert the eternal or the non-eternal", dropping `kāmam` and `ity api` and reading the half as the refutation; it has no verb, and says that, both being established (`siddhe`), granted (`kāmam`), 'neither eternal nor non-eternal' too (`ity api`) would be established, the fourth alternative standing or falling with the third, as Garfield (1995) reads it ("Then it could be established that it is neither permanent nor impermanent").
[^285]: Poussin's edition reads `saṃsāraḥ`, "then `saṃsāra` would be beginningless", followed by Kalupahana (1986), Garfield (1995) and the Tibetan, with no note of a reconstruction such as 27.1 and 27.11 carry; the DSBC's `śāśvataḥ`, "that one would be beginningless and eternal", is kept, since 27.20 takes it up (`nāsti cec chāśvataḥ kaścit`, "if there is no one eternal"); both readings are possible, and the manuscripts would decide.
[^286]: The DSBC prints `saṃtāano`; read `saṃtāno`.
[^287]: Poussin's edition reads `dīpārciṣām iva pravartate`, "proceeds like the flames of a lamp", with `nāntānantavattvaṃ ca yujyate` in the second half, whence Kalupahana (1986) "proceeds along"; the DSBC's first half has no verb, and "is" is supplied, `dīpārciṣām` being a genitive parallel to `skandhānām`, "like that of the flames".
[^288]: The DSBC prints `pratītyemālloko`, the nasal of `imān` + `lokaḥ` (`imāṃl loko`) lost; Poussin's edition prints `imān loko`.
[^289]: As at 27.18, Kalupahana (1986) renders the second half "then it is not possible to assert either the finite or the infinite"; `siddhe naivāntavat kāmaṃ naivānantavad ity api` says that, that being established, granted, 'neither finite nor infinite' too would be established, as Garfield (1995) has it.
[^290]: The DSBC prints the optative `adeśayet`; Poussin's edition reads the imperfect `adeśayat`, "taught", as Kalupahana (1986), Garfield (1995) and the Tibetan have it, and Candrakīrti glosses the verb as a past, so the page renders it so.
